ONE PENNY

BOROUGH OF WARRINGTON. THE WARRINGTON CORPORATION ACT. 1899. TUBERCULOSIS.

NTOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN that by Part 11. 11 of the above Act it is provided that : -

PENALTY FOR SELLING MILK OF DISEASED COWS.

- - Sec. 96. Every person who knowingly sells or suffers to be sold or used for human consumption within the borough the milk of any cow which is suffering from tuberculosis of the udder shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding ten pounds.

PENALTY ON FAILING TO ISOLATE DISEASED COWS. Sec. 97. Any person the milk of the cows in whose dairy is sold or suffered to be sold or used for human consumption with lii the borough who after becoming aware that any cow in his dairy is suffering from tuberculosis of the udder keeps or per- mits to be kept such cow in any field shed or other premises along with other cows in milk shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding five pounds. --------

===== 0002601_18990908_art0003.txt | included=true | included =====
OBLIGATION TO _NOTIFY CASES OF .TUfiERCITLOSIS.

8ee:973: H.Yery dairyman who supplies milk within the borough and has in his dairy any cow affected with or suspec- ted of or exhibiting signs of tuberculosis of the udder shall forthwith give written notice of the fact to the Medical Officer of Health stating his name end address and the situation of the dairy or premises wliFre the cow is. Any dairyman failing to give such notice as required by this Section shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding forty shillings.

===== 0002601_18990908_art0004.txt | included=true | included =====
POWER TO TAKE SIMPLES OF MILK.

See. 99. (1.) It shall be lawful for the Medical Offieer of Health or any personprovided with and if required exhibiting the authority in writing of such MedicAl Officer of Health to take within the borough for examination samples of milk pro- duced or sold or intended for sale within the borough.

(2) The like powers in all respects may be exercised outside the borough by the Medical Officer of Health or such authorised person if he shall first have obtained from a Justice having jurisdiction in the place where the sample is to be taken an order authorising the taking of samples of the milk which order any such Justice is hereby empowered to make.

POWER TO INSPECT COWS AND TO TA/CH: SAMPLES OF MILL Sec. 100. If milk from a dairy situate within the borough is being sold or suffered to be sold or usal within the borough the Medical Offiaer of Health or any person provided with and if requiring exhibiting the authority in writing of the medical officer may if accompanied by a pror eriy qualified veterinary surgeon at all reasonable hours enter the dairy and inspect the cows kept therein and if the medical officer or such person has reason to suspect that any cow in the dairy is suffering from tuberculosis of the udder he may require the cow to be milked in his pretence and may take samples of the milk and the milk from any particular teat 3hall if he so requires be kept separate and separate samples thereof be furnished. (2) If the Medical Officer ofHealth is of opinion that tuber- culosis is caused or is likely to be caused to persons residing In the borough from consumption of the milk supplied from a dairy situate within the borough or from any cow kept therein he shall report thereon to the Corporation and his report shall be accompanied by any report furnished to him by the veterinary surgeon and the Corporation may there upon serve on the dairyman notice to appear before them within such time not less than twenty-four hours as may be specified In the notice to show cause why an order should not be made requiring him not to supply any milk from such dairy within the borough until the order has been withdrawn by the Corporation. (3) If the Medical Officer of Health has reason to believe that milk from any dairy situate outside the borough from which milk is being sold or suffered to be sold or used within the borough is likely to cause tuberculosis in persons residing within the borough the powers conferred by this section may In all respects be exercise tin the case of such dairy Provided that the Medical Officer of Health or other authorised person shall first have obtained from a Justice having jurisdiction in the place where the dairy is situate an order authorising such entry and inspection which order any such Justice is hereby empowered to make. (4) Every dairyman and the persons in his employment shall render such reasonable assistance to the Medical Officer of Health or such authorised person or veterinary surgeon as aforesaid as may be required by such medical officer parson or veterinary surgeon for all or any of the purposes of this section and any person refusing such assistance or obstructing such medical officer person or veterinary surgeon in carrying out the purposes of this section shall be liable to a penalty not exceeding five pounds. (5) If in their opinion the dairyman fails to show cause why such an order may not be made as aforesaid the Corpora- tion may make the said order and shall forthwith serve notice of the facts on the County Council of any administrative county in which the dairy is situate and on the Local Govern- ment Board and if Vac dairy is situate outsida the borough on the Council of the borough or County District in which it is situate. . _

(6) The said order shall be forthwith withdrawn oa the Cor- poration or their Medi_al Officer of Health being satisfied that the milk supply has been changed or that it is not likely to cause tuberculosis to persons residing in the borough. (() If any person after any such order has been made sup- plies any milk within the borough in contravention of the order or sells it for consumption therein he shall be liable to a penalty not excee ling five ponnds and if the offence con- tinues to a further penalty not exceeding forty shillings for ery day during which the offence continues. f (8) A dairyman shall not be liable to an action for breach o contract if the breach be due to an order onder this section.

NOTICE OF PROVISIONS OF ACT.

_ Sec. 101. The Corporation shall cause to be given public notice of the effect of the provisions of this part of this Act, by advertisement in local newspapers and by handbills and otherwise in such manner as they think sufficient and this part of this Act shall come into operation at such time not being less than one month after the fist publication of such an advertisement as aforesaid as the Corporation may fix.

Provided that no such test shall be applied except with the previous consent of the owner of such now.

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAR NELLIE,

There are a lot of pretty bodices about and one I saw yesterday was a perfect dream. It had a large bow of black velvet ribbon about four inches wide fastened at the throat and from it a band of similar velvet passed straight down the front of the bodice, disappearing under a black velvet belt of the same width. This very simple trimming is remarkably effective, and can be adapted with admirable results to any dress of a light colour, such as pale blue, pink, or mauve. The darkness of the velvet not only bring out to advantage the soft tone of the dress, but also makes it extremely becoming. On each breast there is a pretty rosette from which hung long bows of white lace. The breast of the bodice,which opened in the front, was of pleated silk.

I cannot say I admire the bow worn in the front of sailor hats, and one often wonders how it hap- pens that the ugliest fashions become the most widespread. Birds are coming back into favour. Perhaps the discussion that there has been with regard to the use of plumage has kept this mode of trimming in the minds of the chapeau-makers. I know that the girls who make these millinery birds are so expert that they can almost imitate any real winged creature. A handful of roses, hearty, full- blown red beauties is the proper pony for a hat that is to be used with a variety of toilettes, or even more admirable is ornamentation in tufts of tinted grain, ripe russet rose and plummy purples.

A periodical called The Lady had some ifitsrest- ing pictures of recent fashions the other day and one that attracted my attention was one in which the bodice had a perfectly tight back, and the front, arranged in original fashion, with fulness collected at the fastening on the left side. The top of the pinafore overpart was scalloped, and outlined with the drawn ribbon, and the sleeves were perfectly tight. This model could be effectively copied in poplinette, trimmed with the ribbon in a contrasting tint, and the yoke either in white surah or in silk matching the ribbon. This is a charming design for a combination of black and rose or black and lizard•green' using green velvet or rose silk. The silk would be laid in tucks, the velvet plain, and narrow rose and black gauze ribbon would be more stylish than the plain colour. If copied is blue serge, the skirt would be smart with blue ribbon with white lines drawn up into little frills, and the yoke of white ivory, or pale blue merv, closely tucked.

White, which has been so very popular all through the summer, still seems likely to remain in high favour, even during the autumn and winter months says Madqnte. Any number of white gowns were to be seen at Goodwood and at Cowes, and white is still reigning triumphantly at many of the English and for!igm watering-places. There i is much to be said n its favour, too, for it suits most people far better than colours do, and all tra- ditions to the contrary notwithstanding, I shall i always maintain that white s very becoming indeed to the middle-aged and even to the elderly always supposing of course that with advancing years they have been fortunate enough to keep their youthful slimness, and have not lost the juvenile contour of their figures. Speaking generally however white gowns will be worn this autumn chiefly by the younger members of the community, and as very few women nowa- days confess to being anything but young, the tact will probably remain that % 'nite will be very much worn by everybody. A dark-haired girl looks lovely in a pale yellow credo'n, the surface of which is crinkled like the cream in the dairy pans before the milk is skimmed. The mode of making differs a little from that of the great majority of gowns to he seen by the sea. The skirt is trimmed apron-fe3hion down the front with a series of graduated frills. The uppermost is not only very narrow in itself, but it occupies an extremely limited horizontal line. The second i overpasses t in both perticulars, and so on with the whole 15; for 15 there are. The last frill is about six inches wioe, and it is continued round the whole of the barn of the skirt. The amount of work in h dress is still further increased by the addition of very narrow black ribbon velvet to both edges of all the frills.

Your loving JENNIE.

"WOMEN'S CHIT."

As the years ga on, the Dachess of Fife emerges lee and less from her retirement, and is now scarcely ever seen at great funct,ons or festivities. Her two daughters are growing quite big eirls, and would be distinctly nice leokinß, were thetr expr,ssions a little less hau•ebty. That they are not great favourites with' Her Majesty, is an open secret, moreover the Ladies Duff can scarcely be said to be regarded with very greit affectiou by any of the members of the Royal Family. Madame Patti, Madame Albani, and Madame Melba, are each undertaking a conc-re tour of the litritish provinces this autumn and comine winter, a fact which will he bailed with delight by provincial music lovers. Each of these celebrated women have special recipes which they follow, for the care cf the voice. Madame Patti bas a great belief in sugar candy, and when driv- ing or travelling by train usually keeps a small piece in her mouth, which, slowly melting keeps the throat moist. Madame Albani never by any chance permits her voice to become tired. On the days she is engaged to sing she talks only in a whisper. Tea i•he forbids herself, and Bordeaux she finds excellent. Occasionally between her songs she takes, thrpugh a straw, a cup or bouillon, made in the French way, which she finds very restorative. Madame Melba is somewhat reticent con- c,rning her special "recipes" and has never yet given them to the world.

The grouped and straight lines of stitching, so much en evidence on tailor made, and other descriptions of gowns, are giving place to an arrangement of pipings of silk or satin. I have lately seen some gowns most extensively adorned in this way, and the fashion undoubtedly makes for expense, both in the initial cost, and wear. In its first freshness a gown so adorned is fascinating it is true, bat as rezarcia durability, little ran be said. Simple lines, or complicated patterns in stitching cannot wear out, and look well to the last day. Black undergarments are again to the fore, and many fashionable women are patronising them just now. Silk muslin is the fabric chosen, and the trimmings con- sist of black or white incertion and lace, through which coloured ribbons are run, then arranged in dainty little knots. Only last week, I received an invitation to in- spect a trousseau, and knowing full well that it would contain all that was most costly and up-to•date, I sallied forth in anticipation of a delightful afternoon. It was a slight shock to ent-r the apartment set aside for underwear and see black, nothing but bla.ck, every- where. Of course each set carrie I a scheme of colour in the way of ribbons, but one felt strongly inclined to talk in whispers, and wear an expre-sion more suitable to a funeral than a wedding. - - —. _ .

The languishing condition of the British silk manu- facturing industry has been attributed to the tact that it has never been wholly self-reliant upon its own re- sources. It flourished in the early part of the century owing to the fact that the long wars with France stopped the Continental supply. Afterwards the home industry was bolstered up by Protective duties on foreign imports but this did not prevent its s..esdy decline. The cotton manufacturing industry on the other hand ha; always had to fight its own way, and at the present time it is In a far better condition than the trade in silk manu- factures.

Sir Thomas Wardle, the chairman of the Silk Associa tion of Great Britain and Ireland, stated some time ago that it was impossible for silk manufacturers to earn a profit owing to the cheapness of labour in China, Japan, and Italy. But precisely the same thing could he said for the cctton industry. In both cases the raw material has to be imported, and the competition of manufac- tures produced in cheap labour countries met. In the one case these disadvantages have been met in various ways. Greater skill, cheaper capital, better machinery, and fuel, have enabled the cotton naanufacturere to hold their own in the home markets, and in most parts of the world, but this is no the case with silk manufactures, in which British goods are steadily being ousted by the foreign article. It is stated in one of the journals devoted to our sex that nearly 300,000,000 pins are produced every week in the United Kingdom. And yet hew frequently we may be found diligently hunting but for one of these in all the little drawers and nooks and corners, which pro- vide their most likely resting places. The astonishing thing is, where all the pins go to. Everyone know what a bent pin is, butjno one ever saw a worn out pin. They ao not disappear by wear and tear, and one can only imagine that ninety-nine pins out of a hundred be- come "lest." Until 50 or 60 years ago, the mann- fac,ure of pins was a long and tedious process, every- one passing through the hands of ten or a dozen workers before arriving at completien. Now they are turned out in millions at infinitely less cost, the whole of the work being done by machinery. It has often been suggested that a tax on cage birds I could constitute the te4t " Wild Birds Protection Act." Many heitnanitarians would like to see such a tax adop- ted in this country, and they will note with pleasure, that it has been put into force in at least one district in Germany. The Town Council of Apolda in Saxony have dezided to levy a tax of eighteen marks a year upon cer- tain cage birds, not with the object of raising revenue— though it will aleo have thisadvanta ge—hut with a view to checking the capture of its native songsters. More- over anyone freshly acquiring a nightingale by purchase or other meahs,is to give notice of the fact to the Council within 24 hours, or in defaelt a penalty of 36 marks will be imposed. The tax will no doubt prove a hardship to many people who are unable, or unwilling to pie' such a sum, and it therefore seems likely that many birds will forthwith be given their freedem. A short time ago, a woman was seen near London, purchasing all the cage birds on a street hawkers' harrow, and letting them go as she paid for them. That of course is an expeesive process, which not everyone could afford, but most people would like to see some check put upon the cruelty critailed by taking and keeping uild birds in captivity. AN OLD MAN KILLED AT THE CORPOR- ATION ELECTRICTAL WORKS.

Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Warrington Police Station on uesday morn- ing on the body of James Green, sixty-six years of age who died in the Infirmary on Saturday night from injuries received on Friday at the Corporation Electrical Works, now in course of erection. John Green said deceased was his father and re- sided at 2, Bostock-street. He was a boiler maker by trade and was 66 years of age last birihda y. Witness last saw him alive on Monday week and saw him dead in the Infirmary on Monday night. Richard Lawton, foreman joiner in the employ of Mr. Davenport, builder, Stockton Heath, said that he was working in connection with the Cor- poration alectric Station being erected at Howley for which Mr. Davenport had the contract. At 2-30 on Friday afternoon a boiler was being hoisted by means of two derrick cranes. Deceased, who had no right to be in the field wag watching. When the boiler had been hoisted for some distance,the guide rope broke and both poles fell on deceased, knocking him down. He was conveyed to the Infirmary on the police ambulance. Witness thought that the rope was a good one.—A juror : Was deceased warned that it was a dangerous place to stand P—Witness No.— The Coroner said that did not effect the question, if deceased had no right to be there. William Edward Jackson said he was pr,sent in the Infirmary when deceased died at 11.15 on Satur- day.night. He did not mention the accident.

I The "jury recalled the witness Lawton and he was I asked if he thought the rope was a good one. He I said that it was a fairly good one,and be did not see I any flaws in it. It was fit to hoist "teens "of cwts.

The witness Green, interrupting, said he had had several years experience with hoisting, and be did not consider the rope a safe one. Questioned by a jurymam, Lawton said the same ropes bad been used on two jobs, Fletcher Russell's works, and the present one. He was told that de- ceased had asked Mr. Davenport for employment, but had been refused. He had been on the pre- mises for ten minutes or a quarter of an hour,when the accident occurred.

Green said he did not think be could have been there above two minutes, as he was at home at two o'clock, and the accident had happaned at 2.30. He and his sister had since visited the place, but there were no notices about it being dangerous, and they were not warned.—The Coroner: lie had no right to be standing in such close proximity to the guide rope.—The witness Green said that they had no in- tention of taking action; they wanted to know the right, for the protaction of life. Any children who had been there might have been killed.—The Coroner: You might as well fence a pit and some- one walk into it and be drowned. The law does not recognise trespassers. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental Death,' and expressed the opinion that the rope was not sate. Lawton was instructed to inform Mr. Davenport of the jury's opinion, so that if another accident occurred, he would have been warned.

REVENGE OF VALERIE: A ROMANCE OF BRITISII COLUMBIA.

CHAPTER XVII. THE ENTRAPMENT OF HOPE.

It was a mighty wrench to part from her baby, even for a few weeks. But when people have to work for their daily bread, such sacrifices have to be made constantly. Hope was satisfied that she could not have a better substitute for herself than Mrs. Harnbly. Baby Lewis John would have all the care that, even with her love, she could give him. Therefore, she crushed down the latent jealousy that exists in every mother, and made her preparations. These were easily made, and a small hand-bag held all that she would likely require during her absence. When she reached St. John's Villa, she found that she was expected, and at once taken into the sick chamber.

Mrs. Algernon Churchill Champion was a pretty woman of the soft, well-fed and creamy coin- plexioned order, one who evidently liked to be surrounded with every comfort. The whole house indeed was replete with luxury and conducted on an easy and extravagant system. In the kitchen more waste went on unchecked than might have run an establishment four times its size.

The tokens of wealth were everywhere apparent, also of reckless and careless profusion. Costly pictures lined the walls, statues and bric-a-brac were scattered about in every direction. To a lady of such extreme good taste as Hope was, this cram of costly articles did not inspire her with much respect for her employers. They were rich people, and had filled their abode with artistic and valuable objects; but they were too ostentatious in their display, and too careless about their acquisitions to be admired. Mr. Champion was a jovial young fellow, who bore already about him the traces of being a ban vivant. Each night when he returned from business he brought friends to dinner. What dinners they were, too, and breakfasts and lunches, as well as suppers! It seemed as if the dining-table was never empty. The whole house was redolent of meat, drink and money. Mrs. Champion was not nearly so prostrate as Hope had expected. For a patient suffering from influenza she had a marvellous appetite. One thing, however, should have pleased the nurse: she was not a very exacting patient, and soon treated Hope with the confidence and familiarity of an old friend. She had two children, a boy and a girl of two and four years of age. These, however, had been sent down to Brighton for a change. It appeared almost like a sinecure, this post of sick nurse to Hope. There was so little to do, and the payment was so good. She could not object, of course, so long as Mrs. Champion fancied she required a nurse. It was, however, more as a com- panion than in the other capacity that she was em- ployed after the first day or two. Hope felt a little hit ashamed and as if she was an impostor when all she had to do was to gossip or listen to the lady's small talk; read silly novels to her or discuss about her dresses and jewellery. On the subject of the jewellery Hope was at first a little uneasy. With the same carelessness these were left in open drawers and cases, and were so valuable that she felt constrained to remonstrate with Mrs. Champion for her want of thought. "You really ought to keep them under lock and key. It is positively putting temptation in the way of the servants."

"Oh, my servants are all trustworthy," replied Mrs. Champion with a light laugh. "They know at least when they are well treated, which comes to the same thing. They know also that, although my husband winks at small offences, he isn't the man to pass over the deliberate theft of my trinkets. I never have locked up anything." "And have you never been robbed ? " "Only once, and that was by a new footman. The articles were, howeves all traced and recovered, and the thief convicted. He is still undergoing his sentence. Our people know exactly how much we can stand, and never go over the line. And for housebreakers—well, neither locks nor bars will stop those gentry if they go in for business."

"But I would put what I don't require into the Bank where it would be safe, even from burglars," persisted Hope. "Well, you are a peculiar woman, Nurse East- lake," cried Edith Champion in astonishment. "Now, what would the use of jewellery be to any one if it was banked? I might just as well sell it right away. It is one of the joys of life to have them all about me, so that I may look at them and try them on as I feel disposed. The same with my dresses. They were bought to be worn, looked at and admired, not shut up in a banker's safe. I'd be just miserable without them in my dressing- room."

Hope did not speak any more about the subject. She gauged the depths of her employer's mind and exonerated her for her childishness. Those trashy stories written by girl authoresses who had no more experience of men or women than had children in the nursery, were her delight. Hope had to read these crude and nauseous imitations for hours at a time, until her head ached and her soul grew sick with the vapid, flabbily erotic and long extended rubbish.

She tried, on her own behalf, to interest this mindless yet, seemingly harmless, City speculator's wife, in the works of those few authors who are still trying to keep up the art of romance—those limited number of men and women who reverence their art, and strive truly to depict life. _

Mrs. Champion goo-d-natuiedly sent for the books which Hope recommended, but while Hope was interested in the reading, her poor, romance- debauched patient got the headache, which Hope had suffered from over the trash. Mrs. Champion preferred her champagne sugary and her sherry full flavoured; she could not rise above the nursery young lady authors. Yet she appeared good-tempered and considerate of others, if self-indulgent, and trivial. Hope had not been many days beside her patient before she grew really to like the little gourmandiser. Mr. Stephen Belwood came one evening to dinner with Mr. Champion, and both came up for a few moments to the sick chamber. They chatted pleasantly about City matters. Things were boom- ing, they said, and although Hope didn't under- stand what "booming" exactly meant, still, from their cheerful faces, she thought it must be some- thing good. For five days she was comparatively happy in this abode of luxury. Then the catastrophe fel with the suddenness of an avalanche on an Alpine One afternoon, as she was reading that highly moral, instructive and intellectual romance, "The Sorrows of Satan," to her absorbed patient, the maid brought in a card and gave it to her mistress. "Show the man in, Jane. Don't you think Marie Corelli is a great genius ? " "No," replied Hope. "I think from what I have already read in this book, that she must be very spiteful and conceited, though." Next moment a man entered, with his hat in his hand. He was a quiet man, with a clean-shaven, sharp face, and while he bowed, his quick eyes observed everything in the room. "Well, have you discovered anything, Mr. Peters ? " asked Mrs. Champion curtly. "Yes, marm. The jewels were pawned, and have been traced all right. I only require to find the tickets, and I guess they are not far away." "My dear, said Mrs. Champion, turning to Hope, "don't be disturbed. When you spoke to me about robbers the other day, I had already lost some valuable jewellery, and had sent instructions to Scotland-yard." "Indeed," answered Hope faintly. A deadly presentiment was upon her that some fresh troubles were coming. "You will not mind, I am sure, but this officer must do his duty and search all the boxes and people in the house. Isn't that so, Mr. Peters? " "Exactly, mum." "Dreadfully unpleasant, isn't it, nurse? How- ever, it will soon be over. Just touch the bell and have the servants up here. We must leave that naughty Prince Lucio till after we know who the present sinner is, then we can go on with his adventures."

—Hope went over and touched the electric bell. Then she resumed her seat and laid the Sorrows of his Satanic Majesty down, star upwards, on the table. - -

In a few moments, the bedroom was crowded with sullen domestics. The butler, the footman.

page, groom, coacnman, nousemaias, cnamnermalcis Mad bloated cook. tp They delivered the keys of their boxes over willingly, and thee Mr. Peters, with a couple of assistants, went the round of the house. One blue-coated, metal-buttoned gentleman, specially dear to the cook and scullery, guarding the door. Half an hour passed, while Mrs. Champion, always considerate, asked the butler to hand round her own private decanter of brandy to the company. Even the policeman wasn't forgotten. Hope Eastlake waited, with her heart like lead and her eyes filmy with suspense. The others seemed to take this search easy enough, but to her it was an intolerable agony. She had only been once outside since she entered that house. Once for a hasty run over to Clapham to see Mrs. Hambly and her child. Had the robbery taken place while she was away? Would she be in any way implicated? God! the thought was maddening.

She acceptea a half-glass of brandy and gulped it down with parched lips. Would she never be free from ignoble scenes? She thought the servants regarded her with suspicious glances, as the only stranger amongst them Mrs. Champion, however, was sitting opposite, looking at her with a friendly

At last Mr. Peters appeared with his two assist- ants. He drew near to Mrs. Champion while his subordinates stood beide the policeman. "I have found the tickets, mum, all right." "Where ? "

"In thi3 young woman's hand-bag," answered the detective, pointing to Hope. A wild shriek burst from the lips of the wretched woman as she tried to rise. Then merciful oblivion came to her.

When next she came to her, senses she was in prison on a charge of theft. What had gone wrong with her? Some nerve or vein had apparently burst in her brain, and left her dazed and benumbed.

Were demons in charge of her fate, who were driving her to the lowest depths? She began, for the first time in her life, to believe that she was God abandoned.

The gaol authorities were kind enough to her. They had sent their doctsr to look after her. When he saw that his restoratives were effective and that she was sensible, he left her to the prison discipline. A narrow cell with a small pallet bed, two blankets, and the light at one side of the door. She lay down and dozed through the night, not yet being able to realise her awful position. In the morning a female warder came to her door, and ordered her, in harsh tones, to rise, make her bed and tidy her cell. Then she was marched out to a small yard and kept there until it was time for breakfast. Her breakfast was a mug of weak tea and a slice of dry bread. After breakfast she was sitting on her plank couch, not brooding, only still stunned and benumbed, when the door opened and a gentleman came in. He was the solicitor who had been employed in her defence. "Who sent you here ? " she asked weariedly. "Mr. Stephen Belwood, your friend." "Does he still believe in me ? "

"Yes, he thinks there has been some mistake. Inform me of all your movements since you entered the abode of Mrs. Champion." She went over her movements mechanically. She had no longer any hope that either lawyer or archangel could lift the cloud of shame which was over her.

Hope Eastlake had lost the significance of her christian name. She might now have been called Despair Eastlake. One thing only she implored her solicitor, and that was, that she should not be known as Lady Eastlake.

"Let me go down to oblivion as Nurse Eastlake." "The case is very bad against you, but I would not plead guilty for all that,' observed the lawyer as he left her.

"I shall not plead guilty, you may be sure, since I am innocent," she cried, drawing herself

-"Yet the sentence may be lighter if you do," remarked her lawyer gently. "What does it matter whether it is a month or a year or two less, if I am found guilty ? " she said, with a shrug of shoulders and a soulless laugh. She was arraigned and found guilty. The case was almost too clear against her. It looked like a stn'seene, too complete in its details to be quite natural.

et the judge, the jury, and the advocates were satisfied with the stage effects. The past of Nurse Eastlake had been traced up by those sleuth- hounds, the detectives. She was proved to have lived for nine months in a questionable house. The pawnbroker's assistant identified her as the person who had pledged the stolen jewellery. The pawn- tickets were discovered in her hand-bag. While this persecuted woman listened to the evidence she could hardly believe but that, in some moment of mental lapse, she had not done the acts ascribed to her.

She listened to her sentence with a strange and absent smile. It looked to the judge callous and hardened, the defiant smile of a habitual criminal. He wished he had known more of her antecedents, he would have been more severe. "Six months hard labour!"

"Thanks, my Lord," she said quietly, and then she was led away down the stairs from the dock.

CHAPTER XVIII

THE SACRIFICE.

"If my husband wishes to divorce me I shad not appeal, but I cannot tell this lie, I must defend myself when I am innocent." "Very well, Lady Eastlake, since that is your decision • your husband shall divorce you certainly, and repudiate your child, and so shall I, then it may go to the workhouse. Good-bye, I shall not attempt to argue the matter further with you." "Stay for a moment, madam—your Grace, an let me think what is best to do. What is my duty? 0 God, do not forsake me altogether in this hour of my uncertainty." The harrowing interview between the despairing Hope Eastlake and her implacable foe, the Duchess Valerie, had an appropriate setting. A cell in the country gaol, to which the hapless prisoner had been removed after her conviction. Last time the rivals had faced each other had been in her Grace's dressing-room at Falaise. Then the Duchess had good cause to congratulate herself on her superior attractions, even although unknowing that the other had won the game. Now she was triumphant, and the contrast was more striking than ever; sorrow, anxiety, shame, and hopeless despair had robbed that delicate face of its fairness. The prison barber had shorn her golden tresses, and the prison dressmaker disguised her form of all its grace completely. The most queenly woman on earth would be hideous with a felon's costume on. It is in this way that the Government contrives to punish female criminals, by wounding them on the one point on which all women are sensitive and weak—their clothing. A woman can take extra work, or less rations much more philosophically than a man can, but the gorge of the most hardened criminal rises against the garb she is compelled to wear during her incarceration. They become morbid, brooding upon the figure they cut before the eyes of the warders. They get hysterical and tear up their clothes. They become murderous and more hardened and demoniac than they were before in their outraged vanity, self-disgust, and loathing of this corrupting dress. In fact, all that the silent system does to injure the mental and moral nature of the male convicts this diabolical punishment does to the women.

The parsons, priests, and missionaries find themselves sadly handicapped by this hideous fashion. No woman can possibly listen to religious instruction of any kind while her mind is filled with that nightmare. She never forgets it for an instant, awake or asleep. It is the horror and the hatred of her prison days, and it fills her with a bitter hatred and deadly sense of degradation that slay all hopes of her redemption. Before the female convict gets callous on this point she must have lost for ever all sense of order, shame and womanhood. She has become a mindless and loathsome beast. Therefore, I contend, that to inflict this outrage on feminine humanity is as barbarous as branding them on the cheek, or slicing off their ears and noses.

This poor and innocent prisoner, however, had no thought of such small vanities as her shorn hair or shapeless gown at present. Neither did she care how she looked to this beautiful and pitiless enemy who now watched her with such scornful eyes. It is written that the Roman child saint, Agnes, blushed with modest shame, even under the torture, u-lien her executioners tore her robe from her shouders. If so, then her torture was less engrossing than that which Hope Eastlake was now enduring. She sat on her hard pallet, with her thin fingers claspine and unclasping each other her face erev

===== 0002601_18990908_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
;, 1899

with pain, and nor upturnea eyes nieoclshot and burning with sleeplesqness. The muscles of her face twitched in her convulsive spasms, while gonad her forehead seemed pressing a red-hot iron band. It was hard to think, or pray, with that seething and surging in her brains, and that deadly despair petrifying her heart. One idea only seemed to stand out clear—her child. One duty alone called for her considera- tion, what would be best for her baby? He must not be made to share her shame and her misery. Her husband had now faded completely from her mental vision. The Duchess had wiped away her last hope and faith in him. If he had ever loved her she was convinced now that he regretted that weakness. She had lost her husband and her reputation. She had lost her child, for she must now give him up. Now she was asked to complete the sacrifice and declare herself unworthy to hcaa him ever again to her heart. $ll "Oh, my baby—my precious darling! What shall I do, what shall I do ? " she moaned, casting up her eyes to the darkened ceiling and trying to reason the sacrifice from all its sides.

And the Duchess Valereie, the woman who had conquered through that resistless weapon gold, stood over against her, waiting and watching her with curling lips and half-closed insolent eyes. There was no longer any doubt as to the result of that inward debate between truth and a mother's love. Valerie had hemmed her in until now there could be no escape for this unwary victim. She had poured out her money like waste water to accomplish her fell purpose. She had gone to work with the craft and patience of a true Oriental. It was only a poor weak and unsuspecting girl whom she had employed her mighty forces against, so that she took no credit in winning at last. She stood now leaning upon her costly umbrella, with her heavy fur wrappers gathered about her, and on her head the daintiest of winter hats. A subtle and delicious perfume stole from her and filled the dismal cell with its penetrating fragrance. Outside in the lobby the warders were sniffing it up with dreamy delight as it crept through the grating. Glowing with colour, and fresh in her superb young beauty as a dewy rose; with everything that wealth, fashion, art and exquisite taste could pro- duce to adorn a face and form which Nature haA already made perfect; the contrast between Duchess and her degraded victim was too marked —too wantonly and unutterably cruel. Her ostensible purpose of coming here was a charitable one, to adopt the child of the prisoner, and thus relieve the parish of all future trouble. She therefore found no difficulty about procuring admission.

The prisoner was glad also, at first, to see her grace. it seemed kind on her part to come so soon to visit her.

But Valerie, as far as Hope was concerned, felt there was no longer any need to feign a part. In a very few moments, and with incisive, cutting sentences, she declared her projects. "If you have not been criminal, you have been a blind fool. That, in your case, means the same thing." "I am innocent," replied Hope gently. "So every convict says. You may or may not be. lam not here to discuss that. lam here to try if possible to help those whom you have disgraced— your .husband and child." "His child also," put in Hope. The Duchess shrugged her shoulders contemp- tuously, and then continued: "Have you heard from Sir Lewis since he left England ? " "One letter only—a short note written with pencil," answered Hope drearily. "Ah t I have had those letters from him. There they are. You had better read them and then dis- cuss what to do."

She tossed the letters over to Hope, who saw with bitterness that they were of later date to the one she had received, and written on good paper, with ink.

They were short and commonplace, for Sir Lewis was, as we have said, neither a fluent nor a voluminous correspondent. There was nothing in them that the most jealously-disposed spouse could find fault with.

But the damning point about them lay, in her eyes, that they had been written after the one scrap which was sent to her; and that, while he could write three times to his friend, he had not written again to his wife. As she recalled those long months of loneliness and suspense, she felt that she could not condone that wanton heartlessness. This froze her heart.

She read also how he had been disappointed in the gold claims, and that there seemed little likeli- hood of his getting back the money he had lost. These tidings partly prepared her for what the Duchess said next.

"I suppose you can understand now how criminally you acted when you inveigled Sir Lewis into this fatal entanglement." _

Hope hung her hed at these biting words; she felt like a criminal for having yielded to her lover's entreaties.

"You—even you must see also that he cannot have cared much for you, since he has not writter to you." Hope now felt that, without those harsh accents which made her wince, as one does when struck across the raw. Yet they did her some good also, for they cleared her dazed brain a little. She was now listening for the rest to be said with a pre- monition of what it was to be.

"He is a beggar now, and would be a poor outcast all his life, while he was bound to you. It is fortunate, therefore, both for my friend and your child-" "His child also, madam," again returned Hope, looking up, to see once more that contemptuous shrug from her visitor. "It is fortunate, therefore, that your own folly will enable him to free himself from you, and marry a lady who can reinstate him in his former lost position." "You are the lady, I suppose, who is willing to do this after Ms divorce 1" asked the miserable woman, with a swift glance at her visitor, and a subdued bitterness.

The young Duchess drew herself la? and threw back her head haughtily. Then she said coldly: "Yes. 1 will marry Sir Lewis Eastlake, and do my utmost to wipe off the disgrace which his first wife placed upon his name." She intended to be very dignified, but her hatred and passion got the upper hand of her. With sparkling eyes and clenched teeth she hissed :avagely. "Wretched woman, did you know that I low-- him and expected him to propose to me wher came to Falaise ? "

"I thought you loved him then, and for his sake felt sorry that it was too late," answered Hope simply. "Then you do not wish to ruin_him ? " _ _

"No, Madam, I shall not stand in your way." "You will confess that you are guilty as a wife?"

"No, I cannot do that, for it would be false."

"He can get the divorce without your confession, for. your paramour-". _

"b mai-slam, spare me!" moaned Hope, dropping on her knees. The Duchess smiled on her then like a she-devil.

"Walter Gordon, your—friend, has already made a confession."

"Then he is a perjured villain!" cried the tor- tured woman passionately, as she sprang to her feet.

"That may be," sneered Valerie; only it will be as difficult to prove as your innocence of the theft for which you are here now. Listen to my advice, my good woman, and take it or leave it. I don't care how you decide, only your child's future depends upon your decision." Hope sank back on her pallet and muttered: "Say on, I am listening." "If you write out this confession of your guilt, I shall adopt your son, and bring him up to fil7 his position, as tne son and heir of Sir Lew;- Eastlake. No expense shall be spared .or education. If you do not write this confes-sioff, I shall not do anything for him at all. Sir Lewis will repudiqte him, and—he must go to the workhouse meanwhile."

"Why do you wish this from me ? " asked Hope piteously. "Because, if you do so, it will save a great deal of sordid detail in the Divorce Court. No man need be mentioned as your co-respondent, and only a few lines in these dreadful prying papers, which will quickly be forgotten. It will be better to leave the public to think you went wrong with some scion of the upper classes. It is too utterly disgusting to reveal to the world that Lady Eastlake was the w.:mpanion of a Walter Gordon." "Oh! how can I write a lie, even for the sake of my child ? " wailed Hope, wringing her hands frantically. "It is for your own sake, and your husband'a sake, as well as for your infant I ask you to di this. Think of being associated with the book. keeper of a money-lender. Think cf a Walter Gordon "

"What does it matter who it is, if I confess myself guilty when I am innocent? "groaned Hope, rilasoing her head.

-Declue quicsiy, tor my time is up," whispered Valerie. "Your baby as the legitimate heir to mine and your husband's possessions and title, or s beggar and a workhpuse brat." "Enough!" answered Hope faintly, complet4 worn out.. "Give me paper and pen. I shall writ* the lie for the sake of my child!" • Duchess Valerie had everything in her reticule, for she knew she was safe to win. She got them out promptly, and placed them before her victim. "Dictate what you require of me," said Hope passively. She had agreed to the sacrifice of the most precious thing woman retains—her honour. What did the words matter?

Valerie saw at a glance how stunned her victim was. She did not want more than a few words, so she said:

"Write: Dear husband, forgive me and forget me. I have been a false wife to you, and I have gone to where my inclinations lead me. I shall never trouble you more.—Your erring wife, Host WALDBED.

Hope wrote the words mechanically. She was about to sign her name when the Duchess stopped her. "Wait a moment. We had better hare the warder in as a witness."

The sacrifice was completed. In her sealskin reticule Duchess Valerie carried the broken reputa- tion of her rival, and Hope Eastlake, or Waldred, lay an inert mass on her prison pallet. (To be continued.)

ALARM AT WOOLWICH.

CHILD FOUND DE AD IN BED.

WOOLWICH ARSENAL, with its sixteen thousand tkilful workers, has none more intelligent or respected than Mr. Flood, work-taker in the Royal Carriage Depart- ment, at whose home the Kentish Independent reports an interesting conversation.

Mrs. Flood, -a thrifty and industrious housewife, tends her family at 54, Richmond-place, Plumstead. She has a rosy-cheeked son, Herbert W. Flood, who five years ago sustained a severe fright by the discovery of his little sister dead in bed, and the result was a persistent attack of St. Vitus' dance, with weskneas of the legs. Be attended a hospi*al, and seemed to grow out of the complaint until, early in toe summer of last year, he bad a terrible relapse. A cLctor kindly attended him for seven weeks,aNd then sent him into Guy's Hospital. But the lad continued to get worse, and after he had been in the hospital two months he was discharged as incurable. His mother carricd him to London Bridge Station, and when he reached home in a cab the driver had to lift him in- doors.

Mr. Flood's mother saw the lad brought home, and said, "I should give him Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people." The advice was taken on the spot, the pills teing according to the directions for the boy's age. "Frem that moment," said Mrs. Flood, "his strength in- creased, the disease vanished, his spirits rose, and his health improved. You would hardly believe that six months ago he could scarcely move hand or foot, could not dress or feed himself, and was (we thought) on the verge of death. The neighbours all know and are aston- ished at the marvellous recovery."

A shock to the nerves often leaves St. Vitus' dance or some other nervous disorder. Epilepsy, fits, paralysis, neuralgia. early decay of tbe faculties and functions are nervous disorders. The great tonic effect of Dr. Ml- llama' Pink Pills on the nerves is shown by the many eases where they have cured the severest form of ner- vous di' ease. It is easy to see that milder manifesta- tions will be even more readily and certainly cured. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, which are not a purgative, by di- rectly strengthening the nerves cure all that. If a sub- stitute is offered (the genuine pills always bear the full name (Dr. Williams Pink Pills for Pale People) it is better to send direct to Dr. Williams' Medicine Com- pany, Holborn Viaduct, London, enclosing the price (two and ninepence a box, or 13s. 9d. for six boxes). Substitutes never cureA anyone, and are only offered to people who are thought" gullible."

ST. HELENS v. EVERTON. THE STRUGGLE FOR THE CHAMPIONSHIP. EVERTON VICTORIOUS.

Never before bas the fight for the championship of the Liverpool and District ',Prague been so keenly contested as it has been daring the present season, when up to the present three clubs—namely, St. Helens (holders), Evertor', and White Star are all in the running for the premier honour. Accordingly exceptional interest was taken in the return match, which was also the last League match on the St. Helens card, between St. Helens and Everton, at the S. Helens Baths, last night, the fight being witnessed by a large crowd of enthusiastic spectators: During the present season Everton had defeated the Saints in the initial League match, but had been thrown out of the Non- them n Counties' Championship by St. Helens, whilst in the Lancashire County semi-final a drawn game bad ensued, which had yet to be decided it St.Helens. Prior to the most important encounter the appetite of the spectators had been wetted by a Combination match between the reserves of the above teams,with the result that St. Helens won by 6to 1. At a quarter-past nine the following players entered the water under tbe con- trol of Mr. J. Derbyshire. of Manchester :- St. Helens J Aspinwall, goal; J Bretton and T Green. baclis ; P Ripley (captain), half back; J Lawrenson, J Marity, and W Preston, forwards. Everton : C Morris, goal ' • F J Treanor and J S Gomersail, backs ; A L Jones, half back ; C Fsrmby, J Formby, and A E Newcombe, forwards. Timekeeper Mr Melling, Wigan ; goal scorers, T Saunders. Wigan, and G Radcliffe, Wigan. St. Helens lost the toss, and defended the deep goal first. A capital race for the ball was seen, Mavity get- ting possession before J Formby, and Ripley receiving, shot wide. C Formby next bad a try at the other end, but Aspinwall was on the alert, and saved. Ripley and Mavity took the ball to Morris's end, but Gcmersall and Treancr successfully relieved the pressure. Jones next tested the St. Helen's custodian, and then Ripley swim- ming strongly to the Everton end, made a slip, which lost a certain goal. Immediately afterwards Mavity got possession, and showed the audience a capital piece of play, by swimming well up to the Everton goal, and scoring a really magnificent goal. The crowd displayed their jubilation by cheering wildly Thus reversed the Evertonians came down upon Amine wall in great style, but the Saints' custodian, witb the aid of Green, successfully repulsed the attack. The ball was transferred to the other end, where Preston had hard lines. Scrambling play followed, and the &luta conceded a corner. Formby took this, and parsed tn Jones, who struck the croesbar,the ball falling down,and Aspinwall threw away. Everton claimed a goal. and Mr. Derbyshire allowed the point, although the goal scorer did not signal. The scores were thus equal. Upon the resumption. C Formby, receiving, put the ball just out of the reach of Aspinwall into the net, scoring the second goal for Everton. Preston next got away, but was robbed by Gomersall. Just before the interval the Saints pressed strongly and Lawrenson was given a chance to equalise, but Gomenall deliberately fouled him inside the three yards' mark. Mr. Derbyshire awarded the penalty throw, and ordered the offender out of the water, with the result that Lawrensen easily scored, although Morris attempted to save Everton nothing daunted again swam down, and C Formby rc- peated his previous performance of putting the ball out of Aspinwall's reach. Half-time: Everton 3 goals, St. Helens 2 goals. . _ .

On the Change of ends the game was closely contes- ted, Everton offering a stout defence to the attacks of the home team, who lacked the services of W. Walker laid aside through injuries Ripley was also evidently suffering from the effects of his match of Saturday, for he did not show his usual dash during this half. Gomersall and Jones endeavoured to increase their lead. while Ripley, Bretton, and Lawrensen also mode attempts to equalise, but the defence of both tearre was superiAr to the attack, and nothing furtfler was scored. St. Helens being thrown out of the running for the championship. Final result :—Everton, three goals St. Helens, 2 goals.

On Hendey last the members of the various clubs connected with the League assembled in the Cairo. street schoolroom, to witness the preseutation of the League Trophy and medals to the champions, Cairo-street, D.C., Hr. R. A. Neylor, the president of the League, preside 1. An excellent programme had been arranged, several popular local artistes have generously given their services for the occa- sion. In the course of a highly instructive and interesting address, the president remarked, that although he wasn't much of a player himself, he fully recognised and appreciated the merits of the game. He thought the draughts- playere of Warrington ought to be very grateful to Mr. Chalmers, tho generous donor of the trophy,for providing them with a trophy well worth playing for. He regretted that he was unable to stay until the close of the meeting, as he had another meeting to attend, but apropos of his god will, he gave them Jne and all a hearty invitation to come up to Cuerdon Hall. Thelwall, any Thursday afternoon during the next three weeks, when he would be very pleased to entertain them. (We understand that this invitation has been accepted, and that next Thursday. will in all probability, be the date fixed). He then handed the trophy to the Rev W H Drummond, vice-president of the Cairo-street Draughts Club, and presented the medals to each member of the team. The names of those compris- ieg the team are : Mr S Tavlin, qr D Plinston, Mr J Green, Mr J R Grieves. Mr F Wright, Mr A Hall, Mr W Hewitt, and Mr W Tomkinsen. Mr Drum- mond, in accepting the trophy, expressed the wish that although this was the first time that the Cairo-street club had won the trophy, he sincerely hoped it would not be the last. —Mr F Dunne, the president of the English Draughts Associa- tion, was then called upon for a short address. Mr. Dunne, in opening his remarks, expressed a regret that Mr Chalmers was unable to be present through illness. lie thought the Cairo-street Club were to be congratu- lated upon their victory, as they had won it fairly and honourably, and deserved every credit for their achievement. He thought the trophy which Hr. Chalmers had presented to the town, served to promote an antagonism among draught players, which was not an unfriendly antagonism, but a purpose of action which tended to strengthen the friendly feeling which always did exist among draughts clubs. He then read the following letter which he had received from Mr. Chalmers :—Dear Mr. Dunne, I regret that I am unable to be present at your meeting 1;1-night: but you have my very beet wishes for the success of the Warrington Draughts Lesgue, and should the trophy. which I have had great pleasure in present- ing to the League, help to promote a healthy com- petition amongst the various draughts clubs, my object will be achieved. I hope to see the good old town come to the point in Draughts as well as in all other sports and pastimes, with compliments, sincerely yours. W. CEIALMERS.—After refresh- ments had been served round, and a few more musical items had been rendered, the meeting was brought to a most satisfactory conclusion.

- The small diazram will enable our reulers to understand the notation by which the moves of the game are recorded. Black cc'uples the squares num- bered 1 to 12 and white those numbered 21 to 32. The figures thus (11-15) indicate that a move is made from and to the squares BO numbered. Black has the opening move and the pairs of figures apply alternately to Black and White to the close.

AMERICAN INDIANS IN TROUBLE.

At the Warrington Police Court on Monday, before Mr. F. Monks (in the chair), and other magistratee, Bill Yellowhawk and Mack Yellowhawk, powerfully built American Indians, were charged with being drunk and nisorderly in Bridge-street at 6-15 on Saturday even- ing. Mack was charged with assaulting P.C. Monks at tt,e came time. They ph tiled guilty. The Chief Constable said that prisoners were American Indians and came to Warrington with the company playing " On the Frontier," last week. On Situriay they obtained some money from somewhere, to go and b 5 photographed. They did not use it for that purpose, howev,r, but 'naught some drink, but the firewater of the paha faces did not agree with them very well. When arre,ted they threw themselves on the ground, and the officers had great difficulty in taking them to the lock-up. One of the men scratched his face by throwing himself down. A man named Allen ren- dered assistance. but one of the prisoners had to be car- ried to the Police Station. P.C. Monks said that when the prisoners were ar- r,sted they became very violent, and one of the men kicked him • It took lour policemen to take them to the Police Station.

Joseph William Allen, George Evans, and Thomas Thompson gave corroborative evidence. The Manager of the company was called, and stated that prisoners had been in England nearly seven year& and had visited Warrington seven times. Nothing had ever occurred before in any town. They were annoyed by children and others throwing stones at them. He had had to ask the police to pro'ect them at other places. mended the Council to pass the resolution mtking repre- sentation to the Lee ii G overnev,nt Bo tra that it was desirable to conatitute the borough into a county borough. As far as he was individually concerned he had been of the opinion for a long time teat it was quite time Warrington shoeld mtke this applicttion, and some twelve months ago he moved a resolution ask- ing that the Town Clerk sh raid b 3 instructel to prep are a report. That report, which was an ex :ellen', one, had been in their hands for a fortnight. Is was gait° proper that the matter shou d be put as fully and clearly AS it could be, not simply for the purpose of the memoers of the Council, but for the purpose of the general public outside. It was q lite impossible for them to fortell what the re-adjustment of finances as between the county ani Dr ugh might result in, but there was the material fact that at the present time the r ttable value of the borough was pledged for county adminie- trative purposes as well as for borough adminis- trative purposes, and. they were contributing Every year a sum of money for the administration of the county which was only partially beneficial to teem. If they became a county borough a certain scm of motley would be relieved and returaed to them. If they were ever to become a county borough the present was the time. At no time was the prospective increase of rateable value so great as it was to-day. Consider the work of the Council itself. It was to spend somithing like three or four hundred thousani pounds in the erection of buildings, the wideniag of streets, adding to the waterworks, and for that they we'll.' be additionally pledged for county aiministrative purposes if they did not relieve themselves. What impressed the committee most when the report of the Town Clerk was considerei was a suggestion from Councillor Shilicock that every additional pound of rateable value which Warrington became possessed of would be an additional sum contri- buted to the county for administrative purpose.. The Maroa : That is one of our main considerations.

Continuing the Alderman asked what had they to lose by the change? He any re-adjustment of finances, that would be done which was right and fair to both authori- ties. If they had not contributed their share, though he believed they had more than done so, they would have to make up the deficiency. On the other hand, they being the largest non-county borough in Eegland, it would be found that they had contributed more than their fair share,a.nd the probable result financially would be to their advantage. But it was not altogether a question of finance, though that was imeortaat. There was not a man in that room, nor outside of it, who did not took forward to Warrington becoming sometime or other a county borongh. Would they act wisely by allowing the probable enormous increase in rateable value to become pledged in perp ? The only mis- take they had made was that they did not go in for this , matter five or six years ago the moment they reached the population limit required by the Local Government Board to constitute them with the sanction of Parliament into a county borough. (Hear, hear.) There was the qaestion of technical instruction. They had, in that matter, suffered at the hands of the County Council an amount of illtreatment which, he ventured to say, had not teen equalled by any other County Council in the United Kingdom. (Hear. hear.) As far as Cheshire was coacerned,the boroughs had been treated most liberally. From the year 1890 to the present year they (Warrington) had received £5,488 on account of technical instruction, and according to the lowest andl meanest estimate they were entitled to £8,483 They had lost nearly £3,000 by being under the control of the County Council. They did not simply deprive them of the amount of money they were entitled to receive, but they interfered with their management of the classes. They, in Warrington, had not a tree hand. They criti- cised their programme, and struck out what they did not approve of. He objected to the interference. He was a Home Ruler, and he believed that people living in a town understood their affairs better than outsiders. He objected to grandmotherly legislation. The result of the deprivation by the County Council of that large sum of money had resulted in the committee having to go to the Council and ask them to make up the deficiency. The ' sooner that condition of things was terminated the better. County councils and county boroughs hal a separate as- sociation of their own, and they were petitioning Par- liament for extended po vers, powers which at the pre- sent time were vested in the Local Governmenb Board. They in Warrington, if they remained as they were at present, would be in the earns position in regard to those powers as they were now in regard to technical instruc- tion. They would just get as much as the County Coun- cil chose to give them. As they knew, the present Government was likely to deal with the question of secsndary education. He did not want them to be in too long—(laughter)—but he hoped they would be in Long enough to deal satisfactorily with this important matter. In that case it was extremely important that a town with a population of 70.000 should have an educa- tional authority. If they were to be in the hands ef the County Council woe betide them. They would never have anything in the shape of secondary education worth calling by the name. They would be grouped with a number of rural districts; they might have repre- sentation on the authority, and that was all. They would make a great mistake if they did not apply for these powers. Their present representation on the County Council was assessed on the population when it was 41,000, so that they were entirely out-voted. They were even now not receiving the representation that their present population entitled them Co. In other words, the rural and agricultural interests on the County Council utterly preponderated and swamped the interests of the urban districts. Their interests were special and Peculiar, and it was extremely important, growing as they were and likely to grow, that they should become their own masters, and assume the dignity which rightly belonged to them of becoming a county borough. (Hear, hear.) C ouncillor MONKS said if members of the Council had nude inquiries on their own account they would appre- ciate some of the diffilutties which the sob-committee lad had to face in making the recommendation which was now made. What had influenced him most was the fact that, as Alderman Roberts had said, the ratable valuable of the borough was being ',mortgaged twice ever, once on their owe account and also by the County Council as security for money they had borrowel. The further question was that the ratable valise Was steadily increasing, and their indebtedness is the direction he had indicated was increased proportionately. With re- gard to the adjustment of the comp msation he would read the last paragraph on page ten of the report,which was as follows :--•• The Act further provides that ia the a ijustment regard shall be had to the existing pro- perty, debts and liabilities connected with the filament relations of the county and borough, and to the con- sideration that neither the county nor the borough shall he placed in any worse financial position by such adjust- ment." lie thought that was a sufficient safeguard for them in connection with ,the financial inter .st at stake. (Hear, hear.) ' Alderman Bolton rose to speak, but The MAYOR ruled him out of order as he had spoken before. Alderman BOLTON: I move that the minutes be not adopted. I-- 1 , The Mania : Yon are out of order. Alderman BOLTON: I have power to move an amend- ment. The MAYOR Are you going ti defy the chair? TUE EXVNIINEII.

===== 0002601_18990908_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
SEPTEMBER Bth 1899

Tax remarks at Plymouth Social Re.forms and on the difficulties in the Trades Unions. way of getting social re- forms are true enough. Given a subservient major- ity, a collection of items, with no serious desire for legislation, and it becomes evident that only the measures of the Government will stand a chance of passing, and that other matters will be in the way of bills intended to improve the condition of the people of England. The electors themselves cannot escape some share of the blame. They make the House of Commons, and if it be listless and indifferent it is because they have returned the wrong men to Parliament. The Trades Unions are a great social power, and they are always in advance of the unorganised trades. Do they always realise the responsibility of such a position? They have to regard the views of their less ad- vanced brethren and they have to appeal to the workers on lines that will attract attention. If the results are unsatisfactory there must be some de- fect in the method adopted. The educational plans are not succeeding,and there is some lack of soli- darity. It would be well if a little attention were given to this point. How is it that the Trades Congress makes so small an impression on the mass of the workers? It ought to be the Parlia- ment of labour, and all labourers ought to look up to it and to be proud to follow its directions. But, between persons who say that nothing less than militant destructive Socialism will attract and the very moderate who say the resolutions are much too political, the Congress has a difficult time of it. Still, it is generally sound on social reforms, and the problem is to discover how to improve the power of the Trades Unions and their organised Congress upon social reforms. Year after year some resolutions are submitted and passed, and yet nothing is done to carry them into eflect. The labour M.P.'s are not strong enough in the House of Commons to form a party and to influence the side to which they are generally attached. If they agree amongst them- selves to promote certain bills, and some measure of success attends them in the ballot, they have to come into collision with Government business with the desire to take away private members' time much earlier in the session than before, and with the intention to scamper through work so that when grouse shooting begins our apathetic legis- lators may wake up to the glory of the gun and the moor-side. The moor-side has killed many social reforms of late years, and it will continue to do as long as we allow it to be the determining factor in the closing up of the session. A session that began in November,had a Christ- mas recess, and an Easter recess finishing about Whitsuntide, with the House meeting at eleven in the morning and finish- ing its labours about ten o'clock at night would give us as great a legislative output as we could desire. It would be a practical, businesslike, commonsense House. Payment of members would follow the transformation of legislation into a business, and perhaps there would be fewer lawyers in Parliament and fewer of the members who re- gard the House of Commons as a club. In a word, what is aimed at is Democracy. There is no democracy at present. It exists at a Trades Con- gress; it does not exist in the House of Com- mons. Social reforms are only a moderate render- ing of that desire for transformation which would accommodate an old system to modern wants and ideas. The conversion is very sluggish,but it goes n; and what we want is to increase the pace of the conversion, and widen the range of its opera- tion.

SOME interesting fluctua- American nglaudTr t ade with • ons are taking place E. in the trade between England and the United States. They are due to the tariff, to the war, and to the general improve- ment of trade conditions in other parts. As might be expected the war with Spain has almost spent its influence,and never had the effect of disturbing trade in certain commodities. For instance, all food-stuffs have kept up well, and our orders have been going across the Atlantic with great regu- larity. Wheat and flour, cheese and butter, have maintained a high rate, and the ordinary course of the meat trade is now being resumed. It is commonly said of canned meats that they can be bought cheaper in this country than in America itself, because we receive over here all the surplus stocks. This is true of many articles of produce. When there is a glut in the market, ano it is feared that prices will fall, the surplus stocks are sent to this country. We have paragraphs about Ameri- can enterprise and the decline of trade in this country, and the American producer smiles his most pious smile, puts up his home prices, and Prides himself on hist own cleverness. The con- ditions of trade in this country mast also be

Pretty good if our large centres can take in more food. An item which sliews how cleverly an attack May be turned into an advertisement is that of Petroleum. Certain oils have been written down With prodigious exertions and arguments,but they seem to be as popular as ever. -ander the head of Petrolum only there has been an increase during the current year of four million gallons. This hardly looks as if Russian petroleum were keeping Up the -promises made on its behalf. Another alngular point is that we are depending less and less on American cotton. We obtain cotton from India and from Egypt, and we take less from America, without that fact having any marked effect upon the cotton-piece goods America takes from us. One million hundred-weight leas cotton thas been imported from the United States this year, but we have

sold to the Americans 14,000,000 more yards of cotton. piece goods. These are figures which may very well cause our cousins to engage in very serious reflections. Doubtless some considerable stocks were accumulated before the Cuban War, and they may have to be worked out. It still remains the fact that increased supplies of raw cotton reach us from non-American sources. The wool trade of the United States has been a good deal disturbed by the McKinley tariff. It was intended to be so. There was a rush to collect stocks in order to mitigate the effect of the new law, and for a time it hardly affected American, French, or our own shippers, who could put good months against bad. Events are now changing their character. Stocks are exhausted, and the wool purchases from this couatry have been double what they were last year. The records are not as good in iron, steel, hardware, and other things. The competition in America becomes keener than ever:in these articles and especially in the West Indies and South America, and even South Africa. Our individual articles are better, more durable, and more profit- able to buy, but they cost more money, and this often has the effect of inducing the customer to spend is. instead of 2s. 6d., though the is. article is of no value after it has been used a little time.

It is with pleasure that Carlyle's admirers will hear of the attempt to rehabilitate him as a seri- ous historian. The true Carlyle disciple has pro- bably never doubted the merits of the great Scot in this respect. But outside the .disciples there are widening circles of the half-read, the half indifferent, and the purely aggressive, who have accepted with promptness the nar- now verdict of some contemporary his- torians. These worthy gentlemen refuse to. admit that Carlyle has any claim to a place within the charmed circle. Who can wonder that this narrowness should be a tock of offence to the disciples who are mostly young and all impetuous. But youth and impetuosity apart, the disciples have the right to their side. Carlyle was not only an historian but a very great historian. He did not present events to us from the outside and draw therefrom more or less w:se deductions. He pourtrayed the scene for us, and revealed to us the inner life and motives of the actors. Few historians have done this. None have done it so superbly. We can admit with all cheerfulness that Carlyle's " French Revolution" contains many errors of fact. But the spirit of the book is true, the errors springing from a lack of knowledge which was only at the disposal of later inquirers. The mere finding of documents does not make a man an historian. The mere inability to find them all does not place a man outside that circle. The chief point is that Car- lyle was a serious enquirer. His output is beyond all praise. There are blemishes, but no historian is infallible.

The acetylene cycle-lamp has probably been badly used, as many things are, by the ignorant and the clumsy. There have been mishaps owing to carelessness, and to the blundering of persons who have professed to know how to handle the lamp. without having the least scientific know- ledge. Prejudices have followed, and the lamp has been condemned. The risk of allowing the light to burn out after the water supply has been cut off cannot be appreciable, though it may be a question as to whether the carbide will yield such a goodlight after it has been used for two or three short night journeys. What strikes us as vary curious is that the Admiralty should have had the boldness to bring its attention to the acetylene cycle-lamp, and to have forbidden officers in the navy from using these lamps, or retaining them on board ship. Bare science classes acetylene as a dangerous explosive, and therefore an acetylene cycle lamp on board ship is forbidden. The deci- sion manifests a care for the well-being of our ships and their crews, which promises many improve- ments. It may beget fears in other bodies, and hence the matter is of some importance. The bright light the acetylene cycle lamp casts ahead is said to be annoying to cyclists who meet it. A green shade of glass will obviate that. As for the rest, as a magistrate observed in a light case; "The light is for the public benefit, and not to enable driver or rider to see his way." This may not be the opinion of the rider however.

We are glad to see that the equanimity of the people of Stockton Heath has at last been dis- turbed by the rapid up-springing of unsightly hoardings in the district. It is a month or two since we ourselves referred to the disfigurement of the landscape by the cluster of hoardings round about the swing-bridges on the Ship Canal, but the residents in the immediate neigh- bourhood appeared unconcerned. Now, how- ever, comes a grumble, and a legitim- ate one all reasoLable people will consider it. The advertising station at Stockton Heath swing-bridge—which might be likened unto an octopus—has at last extended an arm along the canal bank facing the houses in Fairfield-road, an infliction on the residents which has called forth the complaint published in our correspondence column. It really is tithe something was done by the inhabitants; the way these hideous posting stations are being run up being nothing short of scanda- lous. They are not needed, we are certain, and the Ship Canal Company will make a popular con- cession if it refuses to let any more land in a resi- dential district for such a reprehensible purpose. The Bill-posting Company, too, might show a little consideration in the matter.
