DEATH OF A SAILOR.-FATAL FALL THROUGH A HATCHWAY.

INQUEST AND VERDICT.

At the Weaver -note!, Weston Point,on Monday morn- ing, Mr. R. Dobson (deputv-enroner) held an inquest on the body of Leonard Rudolf Tonselius, a sailor, who met with his death the previous Friday by falling into the hold of the barque " Egero." Mr Frederick Myers was foreman of the jury, and Sergeant Brewster attended on behalf of the police.—The first witness called was Theophelus Bowitz captain of the barque " Egero." Be said he lived in Norway. The deceased was a Russian- Finn, and was an orditrry seaman. He had told his shipmates that he was 27 years of age. Deceased came on board at Weston Point last Tuesday and he engaged him as a seaman. About 9 o'clock on Friday night he was sitting in his cabin when he heard someone moving on deck. He beard the mate call out "Don't go in the hold." —Witness then heard something fall and the mate called out " Where are you ?" Witness went on deck and the mate got a lamp and they both went into the hold. They fourd the doccased at the bottom of the hold, lying heAde the keelsen. Blood was coming from deceased's mouth and the side of the head. Witness telephoned to Runcorn for a doctor, but deceased died before the medical man arrived. The steward and carpenter were on board, but they were in bed.—The Foreman : Why were not the hatches down P—Witness : The hatches were off to let air into the hold.—The Fore- man : Would it not have been wise to put the hatches down at 6 o'clock when the men left off work ?—Witress : There were no stringers on hoard and the hold had been open all day. —William Holland, mate on the "Egero," said he did not know the deceased ard had not seen him until last Monday. On Friday at 9 p.m. he was in the forecabirt and heard someone ealling for a boat. He went on deck and heard someenie coining from the aft part of the ship. Witness shented to him to be car ful he did not fall in the hold. Witness then heard a fill. He got a light as quick as possible and went down the hold and the cap- tain followed him with another light. Deceased was lying by the keelson. His head was smashed on the right side and blood was o-zing out. They carried him to the cabin and he died directly. The Captain sent for a doctor. Be was sober and had not been ashore for four days.—P.C. Bancroft testified t-'examining the holy and finding injuries as deccribed by the witnesses. —The Coroner said it was a case of pure accident, and there appeared nothing further to say about the affair. —The jury brought in 9. verdict "that deceased died irern a fracture ot the skull caused by falling into the hold of the vessel." We understand that the Doctor summoned from Run- corn was Dr. J. B. Delany. ard in fairness to him it should he stated that he at once took a can and though driven there ppst-haste arrived on the scene when life was extinct.

About 7.30 on Monday evening, Mr. William Ellison, of Leinster-gardens, was riding his bicycle down High- street, Runcorn, when a girl named Walton, aged nine, was in the act of crossing the street. The cyclist blew his whistle, but the chill being deaf did not heed the alarm, and the resuit was that before Mr. Ellison could pull up he collided with her and was thrown from his machine. The child was picked up,and carried home to Ellesmere-street, where it was found that its injuries consisted of a bruised elbow and crushed thumb. For- tunatolv no bones were broken. Mr. Ellison appeared to have escaped without serious injury,

Sr. PAUL'S CHURCH CHOIR SERMONS.

Last Sunday the annual choir sermons were preached at St. Paul's Wesleyan Church, by the Rev. Humphrey Morrison, the new minister for the Runcorn Circuit. In the morning the Rev. gentleman delivered a very in• structive discourse from Isaiah lx. 31,"They shall mount up with wings a 3 eagles ; they shall run and not he weary ; and they shall walk and not faint." He said there was something at first sight somewhat strange in the method of progress the passage indicated. It seemed far more natural to start with the walk, and then th, race, and then the flight. Yet this order was true to spiritual life. It was first the flight, and then the race, and then the walk. In the former there was a certain intensity of emotion. This kindling of the emotions was to he found in modern history in early Christianity, especially in the Chutch at Corinth, and in the history of the Old Testatment prophets. In the early stages of prophetic work there was the dance, and physical excitement. hut this gradu- ally gave place to a spirital calm- They should not mnder-estimate the emotiona, but, at the same time, they should not over-estimate them. In the second stage they touched ground. As persnee advanced in spiritual life they became more sympathetic with the world and and with men. They could say in the oft quoted words. "I am a man, and whatever touches man touches me." He did not think there was that kindly sympathy with the world that there might be. The world was far too much the vain, delusive world. That, however, wan hardly the feeling of Jesus Christ. The sons of God had no need to walk on stilts. There was also a danger to relapse into lethargy after special efforts. In the third stage he thought Christians grew more modest as they grew older. They had learnt to say there were certain things of God they would never know. The problems of predestination and free will they had learnt to leave alone. One grand feature of the time was the study of the Bible. It was yet a new book to many revie. Biblical criticism had made it a living message from God. The great problem was " How could they take the teaching of Jesus and apply te life ?" The choir sang very effectively the anthems "0 Rive thanks" (E V Hall); "Praise the Lord, 0 Jerusalem" (E V Hall) ; " Wherewithal shall a young man cleanse his way ?" (G Elvey) ; quartett, "God is a spirit," Messrs Kniveton and Birkett, Misses Crosby and Simkin. The collections were very satisfactory.

A BOY'S MIRACULOUS ESCAPE.

On Tuesday afternoon a little boy aged seven, son of Mr. George Turner had a miraculous esc ipe from being severely injured, if not killed outright. It appears that, as the Or-operative Stores bread cart was proceeding through High-street, the little boy ran beliind and began to swing with the result that his legs got entangled in the apckes of the wheel and he was wound up underneath the cart. Hearing the cries of the little orie the driver had the good sense to stop, or without doubt the youngs:ers legs would have been cut off. The horse was then taken out of the shafts to prevent its starting and efforts were made to get the little boy extricated. It was not however until Dr. McDougall appeared on the scene that this was successfully accomplished. Then it was found th it except a little crushing the boy had marvellously escaped all injury. He is now all right, save for a little discolouration on the right leg. RECEPTION OF THE NEW MINISTER AT HALTON.

_ The Trinity Chapel, Halton, on Wednesday sheltered an immense gatherin., assembled for the purpose of welcoming the Rev. Humphrey Morrissn, who comes to take charge of Halton-road, Halton Trinity, and Preston Brook Chapels la the Runcorn Circuit. Mr. Morrison, we understand, comes from the Hebden Bridge Circuit, Yorkshire, with the highest credentials. The success of the gathering was due in a great measure to the valu- able assistance received from the friends at Runcorn and Preston Brook.

[By A CORRESPONDENT.]

The Urban District Council, on Thursday, con- firmed the Committee's report for the extension of the Runc)rn Cemetery at a cost of about £BO, by which means a decidedly economic policy has been carried out for once. The alteration will be prac- tically the first step in the direction of annexing the adjoining field by the removal of the shrubbery and re-arrangement of walks on that side of the Cemetery; work that would have to be done when ,he field was annexed By doing it beforehand, how- ever, the Council save practically the whole £BO they intend spending, in additional rent.

I do hope that the Cemetery Committee are going to provide further stand-pipes Iv that the grave owners cin get water for preserVing plants upon the graves in times of drought. At present, as I once before complained, the distance some of the people have to carry the water i very great. It would be a public convenience, and also one which could be provided very cheaply. This latter is a lot in its favour.

During the wintemonths,the Library Committee have decided to provide lectures upon "Books and Authors" and it is intended that they should em- brace such books and suct authors that a know- ledge of them and their work will make the general public better acquainted with the literary gems that lie stored in their own public library. The effort of the committee should be appreciated, and 1 have no doubt that it will be.

The fishing club should have the necessary " fillip, " bywhat I hear this week. C3rtain of its members (three) went abroad armed with tackle and bait and taking up their position near to some of the waters recently stocked by the club, it was not long Mote ono of them had secured a bite. The fish was, however, almost too much for him to manage but with the assistance of his brother anglers 191 ounces of fish was safely landed ; nor was this all, for shortly after, another fish almost as fine was landed. This is a true fishing story for the fish were both seen and weighed in afterwards; yet like all other " fishers," these three say they failed to land the largost, which took away both hook and bait. 'rwas ever so!

ALLEGED ADULTERATED ADULTERATED MILK AT RUNCORN.

At the Runcorn Petty Sessions, on Monday, Joseph Walker, of Halton, was summoned by Albert Timmins, of Chester, an inspector of weights and measures, for that on the 31st of July (by the hands of William Whitby, his servant or agent), unlawfully did sell to him a pint of milk, which was adulterated with 3 per cent. of water, and was not of the nature and substance and quality of the article demanded by the purchaser ; also with a similar offence in which the percmtage of added water wee alleged to be 4 per cent.—Arthur Rogers, of Egerton-theet, Runcoin was also summoned for a similar offence in which it was alleged 9 per cent.of water was added ; and Margaret Ellison, grocer, of Bentinck- street, was also similarly indicted, the percentage being in her case alleged as 7 per cent.—The woole of the defendants pleaded not guilty. In the first ease heard Alfred Timmins. inspector of weights and measures for the County of Cheshire, said on Monday last at lit o'clock in the morning, he was at Runcorn, and went to the shop of Mrs. Ellison, in Bentinck-street, Runcorn, and saw Mr. Walker's man about to deliver milk to Mrs. Ellison. Be intervened and asked to purchase a pint of milk from William Whitby. He was supplied with the milk for which he paid lit Then he told Whitby that he was an inspector of weights and measures, and had purchased the sample of milk under the Fond and Drugs Act, and that it was his intention to submit the milk to the public analyst for the purposes of analysis. He offered to divide the sample into three parts in accordance of the Act, to which course Whitby agreed. Than he divided the milk into three portions each of which was sealed and latched in Whitby's presence. One portion he gave tea Whitby for his master; a second por- tion he now produced to the Court,and the third portion he submitted to Dr. Ctrter Bell, public analyst, who I issued the certificate produced stating : "This milk con. i tains three per cent of added water."—By Mr. Burton : Three per cent was big enough of a percentage Witnesa did not kecov whether the season had anything to do with it ; he was not an authority upon the subject —Mr. Burton: But you have heard it suggested. (Laughter.) —Questioned whether the stirring up would not make a difference in the milk, witness said it would make no difference as the cart had to go along the road and the tnilk got thoroughly mixed. For the defence Mr. Burton contended that there were various decisions linen these analyses, but there was no recognised standard of percentage of water taken. Water, being one of the natural ingredients of milk, made it very difficult to determine what they had to answer. The sample was '6B per cent, of the lowest figure shown in the Local Government Board reports. Mr. Timmins pointee out that the certificate was ample. It was a charge of" added water " and not of abstractine cream. Had it been the latter of course the analvsit would have put in the perce tags of " fat:y solid."—ln answer to the Bench Mr. Timmins added that the public snelyst arrived at a normal standard.and was always assisted by the inspectors, and could have a sample from the cows if necesiary.—Mr.Burton objected to any reference of that kind.—The Inspector further pointed out, that although the Percentage was 'iSB above the lowest figure of the Local Government Board report, it was nearly two per cent, below the highest figures queted.—The maeirerates after some consideration ad- journed the whole four cases, for the attendance of the analyst. They observing th.,:t Foam further I widence respectine the "certificates " was requisite before the case could be decided upon its merits.

THE PREVALENCE OF DIARRHOEA.

Dr. McDougall reported the number of deaths during the past month amounted to 56, two of which were in- quest cases, and of the remaining 54 deaths, twenty-six were due to diarrhaaa, three to heart dibease, two to premature birth, three to cancer, two to phithisis, two to disease of brain, two to epilepsy, five to mesenteric disease, two to senile decay, and one each to jaundice, enteric fever, collapse, coma, paralysis, pneumonia, and one to inflammation of the living :nembrane of the brain. Twenty-eight of the deaths were of infants under 12 months. The fifty-six deaths show an average death rate of 37•3 of the estimated population, and, exclusive of inquest cases, of 35.4. Forty five births were regis- tered. For the corresponding perioi last year 28 deaths and 54 births were registered. Mr. D. Bisbrown referred to the extraordinary high rate of deaths from Jiarrhcea, and asked the medical officer whether there was any signs of its diminution. Dr. McDougall replied that diarrhcea not being a notifiable diseae he could not exactly tell ; but he thought it was decreasing. lie attributed it chiefly to the recent hot wcattier experienced. The greatest per centage of such deaths were amongst the aged and the young, and they could not stand the weather upon any kind of diet.

THE CEMETERY EXPENSES.-AMOUNT OF PRECEPTS.

The Markets and Cemetery Committea recommended that a precept be served OD the overseers of the poor for £lB5 for the six months ending 31st March next, for the repayment of loans, and a precept for £lO5 f r cemetery expenses for the same period ; also that the Seal of the Council be affixed to such precepts.

THE EXTENSION OF THE CEMETERY.-A POLICY OF

ECONOMY.

• The Markel and Cemetery Committee further reported " that when it recommended the Council to 3 give notice t) the occupier of the field adjoining the cemetery to ter- minate the tenancy the intention was to use the field as a burial ground owing t 3 the available space of certain sections in the cemetery being almost exhausted. It is found, however, that by re-arranging certain paths and borders, provision could be made for between 400 and 500 graves so that the field reed not 13?, included for a few years to come. It was therefore recommended that the paths and borders on the west and south side of the cemetery be re-arranged and that the border and shrub- bery on the east side alongside the field be reduced in accordance with a plan they submitted. The cost of the alterations was estimated at £80."

Mr. Handley in proposing the minutes of the Market and Cemetery Committee referred to minute (a) (b) being simply automatic ; but with regard to the minute (c)—that relating to the alteration of the Cemetry, per- haps one or two little explanations were necessary. A a few months ago it was the intention of the committee to advise the expenditure of several hundred pounds upon the enlargement of the cemetery, but consequent upon a sub-committ,,e of the committee going and visiting the spot, it was seen that by a very slight re- arrangement, in accordance with a suggestion of Mr. Wilding's. their surveyor, the wants of the district could bc met for some years at an estimate expenditure of £BO. Mr. liisbrown seconded.

Mr. Imison did not agree with Mr. Handley about such matters as the first two minutes, one of which entailed a sum of £lO5 for cemetery exnenses,beity.; called antoma.- tic ; and if they were to consider such matters as automatic, it was well that the ratepayers should be in- formed of it. He thought that the occasion was perhaps a favourite one to call attention to the enormous expen penditure taking place in the cemetery department since the Council had undertaken it. Drawing comparisions between the two administrations, Mr. Imison contended that during the four years the Council had the manage ment of the Cemetery the sanitary expenditure had gone up. During the last four years the Cemetery was controlled by the Burial Board the total expenditure was some £6OO, while during the first four years the Council controlled the Cemetery—the four years just passed— the expenses reached no less a sum than £B5O out of the poor rates. Continuing his argument Mr. Imiann sub• mitted figures which he called uncontrovertible and which clearly showed that the cost of administration was dgher._ _ _ _ _

Mr. Handley stated he considered there was nothing to reply, to. He could only say they had been obliged to meet extraordinary expenses which fully accounted for the increase.

• Mr. Kirkham was entering into details showtug that the Burial Board had neglected their duty by not repaying their loans,and that the C)uncil had applied to the Local Government Board for permission to barrow the money but were refused and told they would have to meet the expense out of current rate, when the Chairman observed that the matter was not open for argument. The minutes were then confirmed. THE TECHNICAL SCHOOLS.-CHEAPER FRENCH.

The Technical Instruction Committee recommended that a class in Naval Architecture bo held, an that Mr. F. C. Dales, of Birkenhead, be appointed teacher at a salary of £l2 for the Feßsion and 3463 oxpenses for each lesson ; that Mr. W. E. Dudley be appointed teacher of shorthand at the rate of 5. per lesson ; also, that the fee for the French Class be 3s. per session.

GILCIIRIST LECTURES FOR THE WINTER.

On the recommendation of the Library Committee, it was unanimously decided that the rfth annual report of the Committee be adoptel. It was a'sl decided that a course of free lectures on " books and authors "be given during the winter, and that applicttion be made to the Gilchrist Trust for a course of lectur:s for the winter of 101. TIIE STRONG ROOX.--.& COST OF £lOO.

The Chairman announced that the Local Government Board had forwarded their sanction to the Council for the borrowing of £lOO for a strong room, he was sure they would all be pleased. Mr. Imison questioned whether it was not the practice of the Local Government Board to decide upon those matters themselves and not to hold a public inquiry. That was, he took it, that the Local Government Board considered themselves the most competent to judge whether the Council should have a safe case for the cus- tody of its property,and did not allow the caprice k,f the ratepayers to interfere in matters of that kind, though they objected, as was instanced some time ago in the case of the overseers when they required some plans,etc. The Chairman said it was so. Mr. Handley also fully confirmed Mr. Imison, remark- ing he was one of the overseers at the time. The Chairman observed it was evident the Local Government Board had confidence in the authority, and did not think it necessary to hold an inquiry.

RETIREMENT OF CA.PTAIN HUTCHINSON, V.D.

The "London Gazette "of Tuesiay evening con- tains the following announcement :—lst Volunteer Battalion, the Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment),Quartermaster and Honorary Captain T. Hutchinson resigns his C)mmission ; also he is permitted to retain his rank, and to con- tinue to wear the uniform of the Battalion on his retirement. Dated 6th September, 1899. Cant sin and Quartermaster Thomas Hutchinson,V.D., hes been connected with the Warrington Volunteer Corps since its formation, and his retirement, after such long and faithful services, deserves more than a passing notice. No man has shown more interest in the volnn- teer movement than h'e has done, and it is hoped the occasion will not be allowed to pass without some practical recognition being shown in Warrington of the respect entertained for him. Mr. Hutchinson, who is also an alderman of the borough,a position he his occu- pied for many years, attended the meeting held in the old Council Chamber in Market-street in May, 1859, for the purpose of considering the desirability of forming a volunteer corps in Warrington. The chair was occupied by the late Mr. Benjamin Pier- point (father of the present member of the borough),and amongst those present at a well-attended meetine were Mr. J. Fenton Greenall (late Colonel of the Warrington Corps, and who was succeeded by Lieutenant-Colonel Ridgway), Major Gilbert Greenall, Major Pickmere, Mr. W. H. Revnoldm. Major Litton, and others, all except Colonel Greenall having since gone to their rest. From 'soto 60 men joined the corns the first night, Captain Hutchinson's number being 24, and we believe he is the only living velnnteer who Was connected with the corps at that period. Ours was the 9;h corps formed in Latina- shire, and it here the name of the 9;h Lancashire until a few years ago, when it was chanced to the less con- venient and lengthy name of the " Ist Volunteer Battalion. Priece of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment)." which it still bears. The strength gradually increased—fluctuating at times, of course, as may he expected— now being shout 700, but there are grounds for hope that b -fore may years have elapsed it will be in- crossed to the more reepec:Able total of 1,000. We have a population of 63,000 and there is no reason why more eligible men should not be found to act as citizen soldiers There are seven companies—A,B,C,D,E,F,G—The last named being the Newton-le-Vvillowe contingent. But to return to the Captain. He was, of course, enrolled firet as a private, and was subsequently appointed corporal, sergeant, colour-sergeant. and qeartermester sergeant, while in 1884 he was appointed quartermaster.a position he held for five years, when his services were rewarded by being made honorary captain. tle obtained his V.D. (volunteer decoration) severhl years ago "for long Her- vices to the corps," and it was a well-leserved recogni- tion. The first uniform was green, but it was after- wards changed to scarlet. Captain Hutchinsen's duties as quartermaster have been exceedingly onerous when in camp, but they have been well and conscientiously discharged, able assistance in this connection having been rendered him by Quartermaster Sergeant Potter, who, it is hoped, will succeed his late chief. Captain Hutchinson will carry with him in hie retirement the best wishes of his numerous frienis.

SPECIAL MEETING.

A special meeting of the Warrington Trades and Labour Council was held at the" Waggon and Horses "Inn, on Tuesday evening for the purpose of receiving the report of the Delegate to the annual meeting of the Federation of Lancashire Trades and Labour Councils held at Manchester. Mr. C. Maks, who was the delegate, occupied the chair, and there were also present: Messrs Downs, Grifflihs,Grealy T Rigby,Leach, Plumb, Willcock,Weaver, it.•'ambrook E Plinston, Ibbot, Fargher, Battersby (secretary to the Filesmiths' Association). and Bullock (secretary of the council.

TEI DELEGATE'S REPORT.

The CHAIRMAN read his report which was 39 follows: " I have much pleasure in submitting to you my report of the proceedings of the meeting held in the rooms of the Y.M.C.A., Manchester, on July 29:h, as delegate from this council to the annual meeting of the Federa- tion of Lancashire Trades and Labour Councils. These meetings are a source of enlightenment to the majority of workers, for they deal with subjects far beyo.id the ordinary scope of a single society. We as members get a knowledge of the different workings of trades other than our own, and link friendship and sympa- thy with all true and honest workings. Great is our work as a Council, arid in doing this work we must expect no recompense, but do it with a love, to bring about a better state of things for the world at large. In reference to the old-age pension scheme, many and various ways have been tried to bring it before the members of Parliament, but it has been sug- gested that the scheme be thoroughly studied by Trades Councils, and then when they are prepared to state or suggest a case to invite the Member of the Borough and other officials in c,annection with friendly societies to take part ill a friendly discussion to test their feelings in regard to the scheme. The Cheap Trains Act was passed in 1883. All railway fares not exceeding a penny a mile are exempt from passenger duty, and fares exceeding that rate for journeys beeweea stations in the Urban districts pay a duty of 2 per cent. only, instead of the ordinary 5 per cent. These conceesions by the railway companies were made under cer- tain conditions which appear in Section 3 of the act and include the provision of proper and sufficient workmen trains for workmen going to and returning from work at such fares and at such timea between six o'clock in the evening and eight in the morning as appear to the Board of Trade to be reasonable. As regards London itself complaints were made and Mr. Lough, chairman of the Executive Committee wrote to the President of too Board of Trade drawing his atten- tion to the railway companies deficiencies. I must state here that attention is being given to this subject in Manchester and the outlying districts and comrnuniea• Lions are passing between the Manchester City Council who have taken up the case, and the Manchester and 8 alfordiTrades and Labour Council and they will insist upon the Railway Companies putting on more work- men's trains at convenient prices or if not take the case before the Railway Commissioners or Boar I oi Trade as the Leneon Reform Union have done. This conces- sion of cheap trains would not only be a boon to the workmen themselves but also a great help to the employers wile have to seek employees from out-lyine districts. Therefore a co-operatioa of em- ployees and employed would act as a stimu! eus ie brieg- ing about the urgently needed reform. The Federation of Lancashire Trades and Labour Cnincils ask you to pass a resolution re the workmen's trains to the Board of Trade." •

NARROW ESCAPE OF A WARRINGTON CYCLIST. Mr. Arthur Wallington, of Arthur-street, War- rington, met with a serious mishap on Monday afternoon whilst cycling. He had gone with a friend to Liverpool, for the purpose of seeing the new White Star liner, the Oceanic, and was riding along Waterloo-road in the direction of the Ctinada dock, when he was knocked from his machine by a horse attached to a heavy dray. He was just able to throw himself from under the wheels, which passed over t,)e front part of his bicycle, completely wrecking it. Mr. Wallington proceeded home by train, conscious of having had a narrow escape of being killed.

BEFORE the MAGISTRATES. THE INEBRIATES' HOMES—ACTION OF THE BOR:UGH AUTHORITIES. At the Warrington Police Court, on Monday, before Mr. F. WARRINGTON AGAIN OPEN WIEI A. DRAW.

notball was in full swing on Saturday, the insjority of the cluba bsing engaged in competition matches. Warringt m's fixture for the day was at the neighbouring town of St. Helens whither they were followed by some 700 Warringtonians all anxious for a sight of the winter game. Though rain fell heavily in the morning, the att.rnoon turned out bright and fine and in every way pleasant. Perhaps it cv.,s rather warm for the players, but tqat WAS only to be expecte i for the time of the year. Tna field was in excellent order, and seemed not to have suff -red at all from the rain which fell, while a goodly ring of spectators lined the fence. There would be about 4,000 people present. St. Helens was repres- ented entirely by last season's players, but there were a couple of changes in the Warrington ranks. viz., Jones. (Huddersfield) and liderryweather (Br r.ol) at centre. Mr. J. Prtestley (Broughton) was the referee, and the teams lined out as follows :

Green, Appleton, Rennie and Cnapman, for wards. Warrington won the toes, and Bate took advantage of the wind and sun fer the opening stages. Traynor started the game for the Saints and the ball rolled in touch. The kick-out saw Traynor again in possession, but he was well tackled by Taylor in mil-field. Bate secured possession from the pack. and passing between Ashurst and Harris led to an invasion in the home half, but Foulkes splendidly relieved, Hallam kicking dead. Even play followed in:mid-field, Traynor and Barnes re- pettedly shining with good work. Warringten made poor use of the wind and sun, their efforts being very unworkmanl ke. Bate broke away several times, but held the ball too long and was tackled repeatedly. Fish showed activity on the wing, and was winded early on. St. Helens played up with clash, ani the forwards after some speedy work succeeded in tackling the Warriagton ' full-back b3fore he could respond. Warm work on the Warrington line followel, Bate finally effecting a re- lief witn a nice punt to cnetre. The scene of operations was quickly transferred to the other end where Feulkea was tackled, and Warrington had a tura at pressing. Their efforts continued to be of a dieappointine charac- ter, though Jones was very near scoring on oee ee.caeion after receiving a pass from Bate. Foulkes barred the way at the corner flag, however, Fish made one or two useful kicks which helped to kelp Warrington on the aggressive for a time. Jones had a shot at goal from a mark by Fairhurat, and very nearly did the trick. St- Helens rallied and playing with dash, they drove War rington beck, Hallam being again at fault. Nice passing by the home backs enabled Liversage to make headway and tnea transferring to Traynor, the burly centre rushed over near the corner and seored the first try :f the season. Barnes made a gooi attempt aG goal Half- time arrivcd shortly afcer with the Saints leading by a try to nil. The odds now seemed greatly in favour of the homesters for they had the wind behind them and a lead of three points. They were early oc the aggressive, a passing movement ending in Barnes dashing down touch and crossing the line, but the combined attentions of Fish and Hallam caused him to lose the ball at the critical moment. "Smack" responded with a bit of his best 'work, Merryweather supplementing the effort with a run which carried play to safer quarters. The efforts of the visitors were now of a more energetic order, and with Bate and Ashurat open- ing the game out in the style they should have adopted in the first half the chances of the Warrington lads looked brighter. St. Helens curiously enough showed a marked falling off in dash. Warrh3gten kept in the home quarter and Jones tried a drop at goal from his own mark and failed by a little, but Fish raced up and grounded the ball. No try was allowed though the point seemed fair enou4h. It encouraged Warrington however and they gave the homesters a warm time of it, Fish causing considerable trouble on the left wing. Scrimmages followed close to the line, Boyle attempting to kick clear but not getting well hold of the ball it was charged down by Bate who scrambled over amid wild cheering from the Warrington section of the crowd. Jones made a fair attempt to c3nvert from a difficult angle. The sides being now on even terms excitement was redoubled, and likewiee the exer- tions of the players. Werriegtoo were the fresher and continued to attack, Boardman being robbed of a certain try by obstruction on the part of Traynor. Bate again got away only to see a nice pass mullei by Ashurst. Doherty made his mark and kicked te the Warrington line where Hallam was foune safe. Tray- nor also got posiession but found Jones a match for him when he got on the move. Warrington continued to have the better of the argument, and when the whistle went with the scores still equal, they were look- ing very much like winners. A draw had to be the re- sult, which was the case last season in the first encoun- ter, though it was that time on Warrington soil.

THE ABOIIINATION OF HOARDINGS' Dear Sir.—Will you kindly allow me through the medium of youripaper, to express the strong resentment felt by the residents of Stockton Heath, in consequence of th 3 poster hoardings; recently erected. The largest of these has just been placed oppos,te property which has been altered and improved Co make it uniform and in line with other hou,e3 erected later, and to be in character with the fine road that runs past. Surely the Directors and Manager of the Canal Company cannot have given any seri- ous thought when letting the banks for hoarding purposes. Hoardings undoubtedly exist all ever the country and one does get reconciled to them sometimes, but in this case it is a different matter altogether. Stockton Heath is the most desirable residential suburb of Warrington. and a good class of houses has been erected for a good class of people. Did anyone ever dream when the houses along Ellesmere and Fairfield Roads were built, that the view of the canal and its traffics (and this was the determining cause of their erection) would ever be ob- structed, and this too by billposters' hoardings ? The residents along these roads should make a deter- mined protest against this innovation,as I learn on good authority that those hoardings may be continued in- definitely, it all depends on the req Areraents of the bill- posters—a nice prospect for the future ! The reason for this is, it is said, that those erections will effectively keep people off the canal banks; vertical fencing would be quite as effective, but of course expensive to the Canal Company. I wold suggest two measures which ought to claim sme attention—A petition from the residents to the Ship Canal Company, praying for a removal of these view-onstruct )rs. If this should prove ineffective, a more drastic step could be taken—absolutely refraining from purchasing the articles advertisscl, at the same time informing the advertisers of this intention ; the advertisements would thus have the opposite effect to the one intended. Tradesmen of the district might also refuse to use the canals—both Ship and Bridgewater—for the carriage of their goods, and inform the Canal Company te that effect. This step has already been taken by two trader- men that I know of. Such methods would be sure to prevail eventually. If a determin-d eff.nt be mad* at GoliCO, these unsightly and obstructive excoresences may very probably be removed, otherwise, there is no telling how far they may be further continued along the bank. —I am, Sir, yours truly, A STOCKTON HEATHEN.

A WIDNES MOTHER-IN-LAW'S VISIT TO

SA.NKEY.

SID RESULT.

At Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday before Mr. Comber (in the chair), Colonel Ridgway and Mr, Ziba Armitage a charge was heard against Peter Phillips. Sankey, of having assaulted his mother-in-law, Jane Drummond, of Widnes. on August 19th. Ha pleaded not guilt}, *led said he had engaged Mr. Roberts, solicitor, but he was unable to come through illness. Mr. Brown objected to an adjournment of the case and said the complainant resided at Widnes, and defendant lived in a cottage on the Bs wsey estate adjoining the Cheshire Lines Railway station. Complainant had not been to see her daugnter for 12 months, and having heard that the Inspector of the N.S.P.C.C. was going to proceed against her and her husband tor neg- lecting their children she went te Sankey with the intention of doing something to allevitte the suffering of the children and to prevent if poasible, proceedings being taken. The daughter was a little melancholy at times, and they loslieved was not always altogether responsible for her conduct. Com- plainant visited the house on Saturday afternoon,August 19th. She saw defendant in the nen cote and asked him where her daughter was. He replied that she was in the kitchen. Complainant said she had been there and she was not. She asked defendant what he was doing. He said he was feeding Ilia fowls, and that was what her sons never did en a Saturday afterneon. He then made abusive references to complainant's late husband, and afterwards said his wife was insane,cemplainant was in- sane,and her late husband was insane. He said he thought complainant was a man, and when complainant asked him what he meant he hit her in the right eye with his let. She was thrown to the fhor, aed he then thumped her on the head, breast arid stomech. When she got up he again hit her on the head. She had reached for a can in getting up and smashed two little windows in the cote. Detendant then put her out into the yard. She left the premises, and going up the lane she tainted and had to be assisted by seine one from a house near by. Wiles she got into town she had to be taken to toe in- firmary and medically attended. She was not fit to be rertoved until the MJaday morning. A warrant was then applied for, and defendant was apprehended. Be was brought up at the occasional court,but a 3 there was only one magistrate present it was decided to have the case adjourned until that day. Since returning to Widnes complainant had been attended by Dr. E:ock. Complainant had no desire to be viodictive, and if defendant would undertake to behave better towards his wife, and consent to be hound ever, sloe would not press the case. If he would not give that undertaking she would press the case, for the assault was a very serious one.—Asked whether he would give such an undertaking, defendant said it was all a lie. He nad not touched complainant. Mrs. Drummond then gave evidence in support of Mr. Browne'e statement. She sald she was not able to see properly yet from the effects of the blow on the eye. —Questioned by defendant, she denied that he advised her to go and talk to her daughter, and also that she threw three peueds of flour on him. Rachael Drumm--nd, cemplainant'e daughter, said her mother had a bleck eye and was marked across the chest and stomach.

_ Inspector Hackett (N.5.P.0.C.) said he had had occa- sion to visit defendant's house. The children were neg- lected, and aa the wife wai considered unfit to look after them proceedings had not been taxon agaiust her. Defendant saw him after the case had been remanded. Be told him he had got into trouble for assaulting his I:other-in-law and asked his advice. Witness referred him to a solicitor. He said he bad already seen one, and he had advised him to pla.l guilty and apologise. He said he would do so if be could get a separation from his wife.

Defendant stated that when complainant came to the house she called him a foul name, and said he was driv- ing his wife out of her mind. He reminded her that a deter had advised him years ago to get his wife into the workhouse, and told her to go away, as he did not want any bother. Be walked into the shed and she followed and lifted a basket as if to strike him. Ho threw her on one side and Fhs fell on her face on a vice. He lifted her up she went to the hen cote, She followed him, using btd langua7e. He locked the door of the cote, and she smashed the windows. She afterwards went away, and was talking in the lane to her daughter for about a (par ter ot an hour. The magistrates b)und deferriant over to keep the - peace for six months, and ordered him to pay the costs.

GWYLLT WAWA. A FORTNIGEIT'S HOLIDAY THEREIN.

[BY " QUILP N."] I have, on more teen one occasioe, been fortun- ate enough to 'else tiil and townlife behind me, and spend a fortnight's holiday in some portion of Gwyllt Walia—the Wild Wales of legend and his- tory—but at no time in the coarse of a compara- tively short existence have I seen favoured with each pure, unneerre eojeyment as attended my last visit to this delightful country. My head- quarters have been fixed at different places on the shores of Cardigan Bay—all of them beautiful—but the one I feel the greatest longing to return to is Criccieth, the small, romantic town high up in the bend of the noble bay. Barmouth and Aberyst- wyth possess some superiority in size and import- ance, and either is hard to beat for the beauty of , its surroundings, but Criccieth with its freshness, I quaintness, unconventionality, and above all its ' natural scenic charm compels me to award it the palm as a holiday resort, where rest and enjoyment 1 may be advantageously combined. 'Twas there hied ma this summer, while the remarkable spell of sunny weather which has made the year a notable one, was at its height. - To get to this remote part of the world is a task of some difficulty, and yet there are no less than three ways of accomplishing it—which is some- thing in its favour as a tisk. You can approach Criccieth from the North by passing through Chester, Rhyl, Llan,ludno, and Carnarvon, or by leaving Chester for Wrexham and going through Llangollen, Bills, and Delgelley to Barmouth, and then up the coast. As a third route you may join the Cambrian line at its nearest point to Warring- ton, viz., Wrexham and remain ou it until you are at your destinatiou. This is exactly what I did, though the journey is certainly a longer one than necessary. The traveller is compensated for the length of the ride by the glorious and ever-chang- ing scenery he passes through. After leaving Oswestry, the headquarters of the Cambrian Rail- ways, the single line takes us threugh valley after valley, by mountain utter mountain, and alongside such streams as the Severn and the Devey until th i coast is reached at .Aberdovey. The ride is no tedious and may be accom- plished with little inconvenience as the Cambrian Reit way Company, under the able direc- tion of Mr. C. S. Dehniss, provides through trains to all of the holiday resorts on this coast. From Aben_lovey, where I have, in fancy, conveyed the reader, the view from the windows of the railway carriage changes almost completely. After speed- ing alongside the broad estuary of the Davey, the train turns a corner, so to speak—and we have the open sea stretched out before us with the waves plashing brightly at our feet. For the most part until we come to our journey's end, the line now runs along the very edge of the coast, passing here through a short tunnel in the rock, emerging on to the face of the cliff with the sea waves dashing en the rocks underneath, and either craggy or heather- covered heights rising to the skies on the other side. At another part .of the journey the train runs throagh a sylvan glade or a sequestered hamlet, the entire route furnishing a panoramic effect which the pen cannot adequately describe. Midway be- tween Aberdovey and Criccieth is Barmouth,before entering which the magnificent estuary of the Ma wddach has to be crossed,an estuary which Words worth says can compare with the finest in Scotland lam inclined to think the poet's tribute hardly adequate, the view up the river from Barmouth Bridge being surely unsurpassable. If you look out of the carriage window on the right you had a most superb view of river and mountain scenery laid out for your enjoyment, and at this moment you could wish the train would experience some slight delay that you might drink in leisurely theVeauties of the prospect. But this is not what the train has to do—but rather hurry you through these pretty places to the destination for which you are booked. However, not to be thought inconsiderate, the Rail- way Company has provided a footbridge alongside that supporting the train,and should you be making a stay at Barmouth you can lounge about here to your hea't's content. Thera are lounges for the purpose, in fact, along the bridge. But the whistle sounds—we must away from Barmouth and its many delights. Still keeping to the water-side we shortly arrive at Harlech, with its National Anthem and fine old castle. The former you can chant to yourself if the inclination seizes you, and the latter frowns down upon the insignificant look- ing train at its foot. From Harlech the iron horse conveys you to such sweet-sounding places as Talsarnau, Penrhyndeudraetb, Port- madoc —the centre for Punt Abarglaslyn and Beddgelert —and at last to Criccieth. Here too we have a castle, mounted upon a huge rock rising abruptly from the sea. The history of Criccieth Castle and its one-time occupiers is worth giving. 'Hs a bloody and romantic story, and for its derail we must turn to the guide-book. "The castle is said to be of British origin, which is very likely, although for historical purposes it may be regarded as a fortress of Edward 1., who is only credited by Pennant, however, with the two round towers flanking the entrance. The jutting rock, defended by two ditches, may be said to form an isthmus, and has been so described by Gough, in his " Additioes to Camden's Britannia." It consists of four quadrangular courts, the first of which is of irregular form, and has the remains of a square tower. Beyoni is another court, having, on the verge of the rock, ruins of two more towers, likewise square. .It is probable that the towers were all of this shape, seeing that the interiors of both the great round towers are so formed. As their archi- tecture, in its oldest surviving characteristics, closely resembles Dolyddelen Castle, Pennant and others may be warranted in the supposition that this ancient stronghold was founded by a Welsh prince. After the subjugation of Wales, William de Leybourne was appointed constable; and, a curious indication of the value of money at that time, it may be observed that the silary attached to this high office and onerous position of trust, which entailed the maintenance of a garrison of thirty men, besides a chaplain, a surgeon, a carpenter, and a mason, was one hundred pounds a year. The charge of Criccieth Castle was afterwards bestowed on a notable Welshman, Sir Hywel-y-Vwyall, or, in English phrase, Sir Howell of the Axe. He was appointed Constable by the Black Prince, who had knighted him on the field of Poitiers for his valour in battle. According to the bards, Hywel was the person who took the French king prisoner; though the honour has been generally assigned to a knight of Artois, Sir Denis de Mosebeque. As Hywel's doughty deeds on the field of battle had been per- formed with a pole-axe, that weapon entered forth- with into his coat of arms. "In perpetual memorial" of his good services, it was ordained that a mess of meat, carried and guarded by eight yeomen atten- dants, should be ceremoniously laid before the pole- axe every day, and,on removal, should be bestowed on the poor. The " perpetuity" lasted till Eliza- beth's days, but no longer; though it is thought 1 that the Yeomen of the Guard took their origin from the eight who were royally appointed to wait upon Ilywel's puiseant pole-axe. It must be re- membered that Criccieth is a borough town of some antiquity, returning a burgess to Parliament in conjunction with earner- von, Nevin, Pwllbeli, Bangor, and Conway. Its early history, shared with that of the whole of 1 this district in Eivionydd, is particularly turbulent. The county was possessed by two clans—one des- cended from Owain Gwynedd, Prince of North 1 Wales, and consisting of the houses of Cesail Gyvarch, Ystum Cegid, Clenenney, Brynkir, and Glasfryn or Cwm-ystrallyn ; the other derived from Collwyn ap Tango, and comprising the houses of Chwilog, Bron-y-foel, Berkin or Abercaiu, Gwyn- vryn, Tal-hen-bont (now Plas-hen), and Pennardd. The feuds of these two parties filled the land with blood, and they record of their time was one dreary tale of revenge, perfidy, and slaughter. This con- sideration induced Meredydd ap Ivan, ancestor of the Wynne family. of Gwydir, to quit his paternal country. "I had rather," said he," fight with out- laws and thieves than with my own blood and kindred, or be killed by them." They would quarrel "for the first good-morrow," says Sir John Wynne. 1 It was for no better cause that John Owain ap John ap Meredvdd and Hywel ap Madoc Vychan came to strife. *When Hywel fell, his mother placed her hand on his head to prevent the fatal blow,and had her fingers cut off by some of her nearest kindred. An attempt was made to kill Hywel ap Rhys in his own house by the sons of John ap Meredydd for no other reason than that their servants had quarrelled about some fishing! and the Meredydds set fire to the mansion of their foe with great bundles of straw."

Criccteth should be a lively place, one thinks, after perusing this brief account of the doings of thoee turbulent chieftains who once dwelt here. But the character of the natives has changed since then, and changed very much for the better. They still gather round the sea-front, and gaze reflec- tively over the blue waters, but their thoughts are not the thoughts of.their forefathers a century ago.

Than, the hardy folk of Criccieth bore some small reputation as wreckers, and woe-betide any strange vessel which on a dark and stormy night entered Tremedoc bay. The guiding !ight which they would observe on the coast spelt not safety, but disaster. I imagine that the heighe and prominence cf Castle. Hill were features of coesidereble useful- ness to the wreckers of Criccieth in these by-gone times. Many a stark ship must have come to grief against the rocks which now afford such an ileal vantage ground to the visitor whose energy is only sufficient to ensconce him here with a pipe and b ook as cempaniens. Reflects, frowning at you across the bight, looks astonishingly near, and in the back- ground are the dark masses of Ceder Idris, and the smaller peaks which always cluster round one of nature's mountain giants. I have lingered many an hour on Castle Hill absorbing the beauties of the scene. But in the fortnight's holiday I have pre- viously alluded to, this was by no means the only thing done during the many hours in which I had nothing more troublesome to think of than the means and methods for y own enjoyment. I was not"alone in this strange land." There was a gay party of us, numbering near a dozen, and many were the feats we performed. Once we started in the cool of early morn, trained to Portmadoc, and nere exhibited a fine indepead- ence of the coaches which run in conjunction with the Cambrian trains and convey the tourist to Bedd-Gelert —Geleree grave—through the pass of Aberglaslyn. On occasions such as this was, we were tourists in both name and nature. The con- venience of caches possessed no attraction for us. We set out to tramp and tramp we did, taking our own time, stopping where we liked, and obtaining much more enjoyment from the journey than the l'izy mortals who resorted to the conveyances. It amused Us to see the looks of pity and compassiou directed at us by the occupants of the c )aches,when one happened to pass Us en route. Teey marvelled at our method of enjoyment—we marvelled still more at their's, and were content in our confidence of superiority But I digress. We tramped to &Ad- ' Gelert, admired to the full the romantic pass which we traversed aced had lunch at the village named after the grave of one po )r hound, who, treste I un- justly in life hath been iinne nealised in death. We would have wept had it been poseible, but like Artemus Ward in the tower of London we could not shed tears over remains which had been dead so many hundred years. We looked as pathetic as possible iind in this frame of mind were caught by seme enterprising youngsters retailing photographs of Gelert's sepulchre. One of our party with a callous humour deserving reproof offered to pur- chase a" photo" of Gelert if the lad had one, but this was more than he could supply. But if there be any enterprise in Bedd-Gelert, I fully expect that a lifelike presentment of the faithful hound will ere now be prepared to meet the der.eand of the next inquisitive teurist. Leaving this lovely, mountain girt glen, we plodded onwards through Nant Gwynant Vale under a tropical sun to the head of Llanberis Pass, where a brief halt was made for much needed refreshment. Then down the Pass we went, between beetling crags and frowning precipices to felanberis, whence we took train to Criccieth, and the goo Ily feast prepared by our hostess at " Rhianfa." The 22 mile walk we had accomplished was worthy of the effort indeed, and though one or two lady mem- bers of the party were footsore and weary, none regretted having undertaken the tour. This was our most extended excursion on foot. Snowdon and Crider Idris were bete ascended, but the heat haze which hung over the mountains prevented an extensive view being obtained. After such a trip as thos- I have enumerated a day or two was spent in recuperation and laziness. Boating, canoeing, and , bathing were quieter pleasures which occupied the time agreeably. The fierce heat of "Old Sol" made the last-named occupation a necessity of frequent recurrence. Nothing more enjoyable in a physical sense have I ever experienced. With the water warm, clear as crystal,and calm as a duck-pand,one could swim about with an ease and comfort really remarkable. It is no exaggeration to say that the visitors during the time they were at Criccieth be- came actual amphibians. About the numerous charming walks in the immediate neighbourhood I need say little. A place so admirably situated as Criccieth must needs have surroundings of the most attractive kind, and in exploring them many a plsasant hour was spent. Only a mile and a half away are the Black Reck,and a number of fine caves honeycombing the face of the cliff, which is here- abouts very precipitous. When the tide is out the caves can be visited on foo', and well repay the journey, though perhaps they are the more impres- sive when entered by boat at dusk. Then, with the waves washing in and out, and arousing all sorts of sepulchral groans and hisses the experience is de- cidedly striking. There is a legend which asserts that " once upon a time"—oh, these legends ! two pipers entered a cave and losing their way in the gloom, went on and on until they emerged in the Pennant Valley some half dozen miles off. Furthermore, the said pipers played valiantly on their instruments the while. accasion- ally varying the tune with" Cri-certh "—a cry of distress, from which the name Criccieth is alleged to have been derived. Of course, nobody needs to believe a legend if he does not want. They are created, usually without any apparent foundation on fact, for the sole purpose of giving narrators and chroniclers something to talk about and stimu- late interest. A good liar, you may know, is well worth listening to at times. He and the legend-monger are near related. This legend is just as unreliable as any other. Not one at the caves betrays the remotest indication of having ever penetrated more than the modest dis- tance of 20 yards or so into the bowels of Mother Earth. These stubborn facts are ignored by the real,hoary legend of antiquity—wherein lies its own peculiar and distinctive charm. Leaving Criccieth in the oppesite direction, and about the same dis- tance away, is Llanystumclwy, a small village nest- ling under a large name. One of " Gvvyllt Walla's.' most striking characteristics is that the smallest hamlets have the largest and most awe-inspiring names. On one part of the Cambrian system the traveller halts at a station groaning under the weight of:such a tongue-twister as Llanflhangelgeneu'rglyn. Usually, he gazes compassionately at the porter reeling this jumble of consonants and vowels off with unintelligible fluency, shudders and passes on —to the next constituency with a jaw-breaking title. The village here cited, I believe would, if stretched out in one direction, just about reach two- thirds of the way down the string of syllables and other things forming its name. Such is the pleas- ant system Wales is built upon. Llanystumdwy, however, is not quite so atrocious, and moreover possesses the redeeming merit of being an exceed- ingly picturesque place. The Dwy-fawr river flaws through it, and a ramble up the water-course is one of the most delightful things imaginable. The water tumbles over boulders and stones—so doss the pedestrian—while here and there is a deep pool almost screened from observation by the close-woven foliage overhead. But "All things have an end," and this article, like the holiday, must draw to a close. I regret almost as much to leave the scenes now conjured up by memory, as I did when they and the parting were actual and real, but time and space put a limit on my wanderings. Hark w:eat Was that ? A still small voice at my elbow says "Copy!" 'Tis the "P. 0. that insatiable sprite whose demands must be obeyed, but can never be satisfied. He seizes his prey and flies, and I, perforce, must say " Adieu."

THE EXAMINEk, FRIDAY, SEPTEMBER 8, 199

WARRINGTON MARKETS.

At the Warrington Corn Market on Wednesday new wheat "(white) fetched 3s 9d, and old wheat (white), 33 101 and 3s lid (701 b. to the bushel); old red wheat, 3s 101, new red wheat, 3s 81 ; oats, 24 and 24 Id. Flour, best seconds, per 2401 b., 183 Od. Bran, 101 and lid per 201 b. At the Provision Market prices were as follow:—Fresh bu:-ter Is 31 and Is 41 per lb.; fresh eggs, 8 and 9 for ls; fowls. 4s 61 to 5s 6il per couple; English beef, 5d to 10d; lamb, 7d to 10d; mutton 61 to 10d; veal, 801 to;11d; pork, 6d to 81. SCHOOL TREAT AT DARESBURY.

The annual treat provided out of the proceeds of a concert arranged by the master of the Daresbury School was given to the scholars attending the Daresbury,Moore,Hatton,and Preston Brook Schools on Friday. About 300 were present, those from Moore being conveyed to Daresbury by Messrs J L Jones and G W Millington, those from Hatton by Mr It Warburton, and those from Preston Brook by Mr P Tickle. Upon their arrival at Daresbury about two o'clock a field was placed at their disposal by Mr. Joseph Billington, and here games of all kinds were indulged in until four o'clock, when an excel- lent tea wai partaken of in the school. After tea games were again resumed, swingboats, &c , receiv- ing a large amount of patronage. About dusk the proceedings terminated, nuts, sweets, anri cakes being distributed to each scholar. Many ladies be- sides teachers willingly assisted in the preparation of tea and amusements,and to these thanks are due from the scholars.

The minutes were passed on the proposition of Mr. ColEngwool seconded by Mr. Evans. MEDICAL OFFICERS REPORT.

The report of the Medical Officer Of -Health for the quarter ended June 30:h was read before the Highways and Sanitary Committee and adopted. Dr. Watkins re- ported that there were registered 160 births ani 59 deaths. The birth rate was 423 and the death rate was 15.6 per 100'0, of the estimated population. The infantile death rate was 139 per cent. The deaths under 5 years of age were 32 2 per cent of the total number of deaths. During the quarter 72 cases of scarlet fever were noCfied, but fortunately the character of the epidemic was of a mild type, only one out of the 72 cases being fatal. In the last month of the first quarter of the year there were only 7 cays of the disease notiflei. In April the numb3r was 20, in May 37 (the culminating point), in Juna 15, ' From July let to 6:h nine cases were notifled,and thence to thetime of writing(Juiy 21) no case had been notified. The distribution of the zymotie cases was—Town Hall Ward, 5 ; Viaduct Ward, 13; Crow-lane Ward. 14 ; War- grave Ward, 35; Newton Ward, 11; total. 78. Thus the zymotic sick rate (notified cases) was 5.1 per 1,000 of the estimated population- The deaths certified from zymotic diseases were scarlet fever, and diphtheria (notifiable and notified), 1 each, measles and whooping cough (non-notifiable), 2 of the former and 3 of the latter. Of the 34 cases of scarlet fever notified from Wargrave Ward. 19 occurred in Vulcan Cottages. He could not account for this markedly increased local in- cidence of the disease, unless it was that the close pre v- mity of the cottages to the Newton Brook exposed the dwellers in them to the noxious emanations from the brook—which was here very bad, and so their standard of health was lowered, and their power of resistance to disease lessened. The other 15 case; in the ward were pretty evenly distributed over the rest of the ward. It had been a most serious misfortune that since the 18th April last our power of isolation has been almost abro- gated (from April 18 to May 16 it was so entirely). Only two cases were admitted in May,and one in June, to the isolation hospital. In the whole quarter only ten cases of scarlet fever were admitted. GENER 1.1., PURPOSES COMMITTEE.

He said there was a great want in the district of more direct roads between various parts of the district. There had been a gradual growth in the Earlestown, Newton, and Wargrave districts and as far as he could see this growth was still increasing—he might say rapidly increasing. They had been providing for this growth in gas and water extensions but as far as he could see no definite or systematic plan had been laid down for the extension of roais between outlying parts: Small colonies of buildings had sprung up in various places but viewed as a whole they presented a very higgledy-piggledy appearance. Thera was a great need of the question being tackled in a more general spirit and on more deliberate lines. At present in certain part 3 they had a perfect maze of streets without through communication and in some cases they were partially barricaded. There seemed to be no desire to view the town as a whole. Each wan i had been worked out sectionally. THE LATE MIL JAMES JOHNSON.

FUNERAL SERMON AT PRESTON BROOK.

On Sunday evening, a large congregation assembled at Preston Brook Chapel, drawn together in bonds of sadness, by the great loss the chapel has sus- tained by the removal of the late respected saperin- tendent of the Sunday school, Mr. James Johnson, whose death was recorded last week. The preacher was M 7. R. H. Paulette of Runcorn, and he founded his re- marks upon the first few verses of St. John xiv., " Let not your hearts be troubled : ye believe in God, believe also in Me. In my Father's house are many mansions ; if it were not so, I would have told you. Igo to pre- pare a place for you." Having spoken of the text as the most beautiful passage of Scripture—one so beauti- ful that the young and the old both grasped its full meaning, and could neither add nor abstract anything, or set forth anything that the text itself did not most fully and beautifully show,Mr. Posnitt went on t 3 show that the word •‘ mansion" in the original meant a "place of refreshment"—where one could refresh himself whilst upon a journey, and it was this which suggested to theologians the idea of degrees in heaven. Not being a theo!ogian, however, he would net follow that thought any further. He only wished to point out, how, in the life of him who had so recently been taken away from them,;this " mansion " this place of refreshment had ever been his stronghold. His faith was foundsd upon the rock-bed of the Lord Jesus Christ and was firm to the end. At the conclusion of a most eloquent dis- course Mr. Pcsnett added it was his intention t 3 read to them a few of the items that went to make up their departed brother's career :—"James Johnson was born in Runcorn in May, 1815. In due course he attended Brunswick School as a scholar, until, on attaieing the age of 15, be was appointed a teacher of a junior class. A year later, aged 16, he became a member of society, and joined the class, and had been a faithful member in Christ for the last 67 years. That year he was appren- ticed to his tradeeand, when he came out of his time he went to Liverpool to earn his living. Whilst at Liver- pool he usod to worship at the Old Pitt-street Caapel. At the end of two or three years' time he returned from Liverpool to Runcorn and shortly afterwards returaed to Preston Brook (about 1840,) since which time—with the exception of two years healed closely identified himself with that place of worship. Brother Johnson had been connected with the Sunday School of that place since 1844, and had been superintendent for upwards of 50 years and also a class leader for a like period or longer, and with the excep- tion of the two years already mentioned he was most intimate with the church up to the hour of death. It would be in the year 1871 that Mr. Johnson removed to Runcorn, and in that connection the preacher read the following interesting letter.whicts had been found amongst Mr. Johnson's papers after his death. The letter headed spoke more fully that he could of the love ami esteem in which he was held :—" Pres- ton Brook, July 26th, 1871. My dear old friend.— Having heard of your expected removal from our little village, I cannot resist writing to you prior to your de- parture. Do not my old friend think that this letter is written with a view to interfere with your private matters, but kindly view it as a token of respect from one who has, heard and can well imagine the loss which your Sunday School and the village at large would sustain by your absence. For a moment let your thoughts ramble to the days which have passed and you will find that many of your old Sunday school scholars can now look back with pleasure upon the kindness displayed towards them by you, and some can also attribute to you the first sowing of spiritual good. How dark is the present—one who is loved by young as well as old is to leave the seat of his first labours, and those who he has now under his kind care training for a better country, wonder who can fill his place? It seems a simple question, but it cannot be answered. Friend, canst thou leave thy flock, and be the means of them getting astray ? Thy soothing words have been in our village, the means of shedding many a glimmer of hope to the widowed wife. Thy bright smile has been the sunlight of the fatherless child. Thy prayers at many a death-bed have been the means of pointing a creature to his God. Thy solemnity of spirit has been the means of shewing many a sinner the awfulness ot a Godless life. Thy kindly heart has reaped for thee that respect which few men can lay claim to, viz., that your contemplated loss is felt by one and all. What honours for a man! And canst thou leave? Stay. That arm which once knew not its strength is somewhat blighted by the winter of life. Rest is required for thy weary body. Rest with us ;as thou hast lived.so die—the respected of all. With every wish that you may continue amongst us, I remain, yours anonymously, a lover of the veteran superintend- ant."—Truly, continued the preacher,if he was a veteran in '7l what must he have been 20 years afterwards, when a spotless life at which no one could throw a stone was brought to a close in peace. He remembered seeing him on his death bed, and sat with him reading the 121st Psalm, but so strong and thorough was their dead brother's hold of the Scripture, that he was reciting it himself and leading the reader before many verses had been read. Finally, Mr. Posnett concluded with an appeal for volunteers to continue the work so stead- fastly carried on by Mr. Johnson, for whom they mourned as for the loss of one whose place could not be " Elijah wa3 dead ! Who was there to take up his mantle ?"—During the service the favourite hymns of the deceased were sung.

AM INER WORKS, .REWSEY STREET, WARRINGTON.

A RUNCORN BOY KILLED,

TERRIBLE FATALITY.

The Deputy-coroner Mr. R. Dobson, held an inquest at Runcorn Potice Court, on Monday morn', upon the body of Harry Pye, the eight year old son of Mr Mathias Pie, 13, King-street, who met his death by being accidentally run over by a waggon in the vicinity of Ferry Hut, near the Manchestsr Ship Canal.—Mr Joseph Burton, solicitor, Runcorn, appeared for the Ship Canal Company, and Messrs F Wiswall (engineer) and Captain Heasley were also present. The evidence adduced was as follows :

Mary Jane Pye, wife; of Mathias rye engineer. of 13, King-street, Runcorn,stated that the uody the jury had bean viewing was that of her son Harry Pie, agea eight. She last saw him alive in Cooper-street whNe he asked her for a half-penny. Ste gave him the coin and he wont off. About 10 minutes past 7 o'clock he was brought home very much injured. She put him to bed and re- mained with him until nearly one o'clock when he died. Dr. Robinson who was summoned gave her to understand it was a hopeless case from the first. She could herself see that the boy was injured, his left leg being cut open, and there being severe bruises about the stomach— (abdomen)—but the skin was not ruptured. The boy asked her to kiss him. She did and asked him what be had been doing, be replied " Peter Bostock bad done it by shoving the waggon." Peter Bostock said he was the son of Mr. James &s- -tock, fiatman, of 16, Alcock-street, Runcorn. Last Saturday evening he went in company with two other boys named Kirkham and Woods with the intention of catching crabs in the Ship Canal. They, however, saw a number or children playing with a waggon on the siding belonging to the Ship Canal Company, and went and joined them. He saw Harry Pye, whom he knew well, underneath the waggon " tossing on the axle." As a lot of children were trying to shove the waggon along witness called " Harry come from under." Deceased replied " I shan't." Witness and another boy named Thomas Smith then tried to push against the chil- dren who were pushing the waggon down the slope, but finding they could not stop it witness said he would leave go, and shouted to Smith to put a piece of wood in front of the wheel. Smith did not, but another boy did. The waggon, however, went over the piece of wood, and it was not until the witness "spragged" the wheel properly that the waggon stopped. The deceased had been run over by the waggon, as far as witness could tell it appeared to go right up his back. Only one wheel went over deceased. Witness then helped to extricate him and to carry him home. He carried deceased part of the way himself, He wanted to take deceased for a drink of water,and also suggested that be should be taken to a doctors,but the others pre- vailed and he was taken homa.—By Mr. Burton : There was a fence to prevent people getting to the siding but he went through not over it. The waggon would be about seven yards from the fence at the time of the acciuent.—Tne foreman : At the time of the accident was the waggon loose ? Witness t It was and anyone could shove it about.—The Coroner: Did you know you were doing wrong by going on the siding?—Witness: I did not know I was doing wrong, sir ; I was going to catch crabs.—A juror : Was there no sprage in the waggon when you first got there P—Witness: They were playing with the waggon when we got there.— the juror: You could not tell whether it was seven or 50 yards to the siding from the fence P—Witness : No, I only guessed. Thomas Smith, said he was the 12 year old son of Mr. Thomas Smith, 10, Cooper-street, Runcorn. He knew Harry Pye and saw him under the waggon but did not know how he got there. With the assistance of John Tickle he got Pye out from under the waggon and helped to take him home. First one and then the other carried him. Pye was suffering very much. There were a number of boys and girls pushing the waggon one way. —ln answer to numerous questions witness stated he saw no spragg in the waggon before the accident. There was only one waggon that the children were playing with.

Thomas Berrington. of Weston Point, stated he was a wheelwright in the employ of the Manchester Ship Canal Company. Near to the siding where the accident occurred there was a repairing shed where he worked. On Saturday he was down there, and there w, re three waggons undergoing repairs. One was inside the shed and the other two were on the siding, He left off work at 12 &clock. Before that time however, he had with his assistant put the waggons into a position of safety,by placing the two together. He then got a piece of stout plank about 6 to 8 feet in length and inserted it in the aide of the waggon, having jammed down Vivi brake before doing so. He then got a screw bolt and nut and used them for keeping the brake in position in place of the ordinary brake pin.—By the jury : He left the waggon in such a state that it would be almost impos- sible for boys t 3 move it. It was a mystery to him h3cv they had got the bolt loose. The foreman : I should think so.

Mr. Francis Wiswall, engineer of the Bridgewater Canil Company, gave evidence as t 3 knowing the spot where the accident happened and of a nature tending to show that the children were trespassing on the siding which was a private one. The Company had hard work to keep the fences in repair as they were being continually broken down. The jury brought in a verdict of "Accidental death " duo to being run over by a waggon.

RUNCORN'S FORM AT WIGAN.-ROOll

FOR IMPROVEMENT.

[By THE " Sconcaßn."] As I anticipated, the game at Wigan, last Satur- day, resulted in the registration of the first 2 points for Runcorn in the "Great League," and a signal victory was obtained over the Wiganites, both matters for great congratulation. At the same time, better form will have to be shown before the Club's followers can become filled with confidence and enthusiasm. Of course, in saying this, I make the due allowances for the first "turn-out," and also for the fact that the team may have realised from the first that the issue of the game was never in doubt. Sam Houghton caused me a deal of anxiety in the first quarter, and after all I was beginning to think the Isle of Man trip was not RO beneficial as hoped ; but in the latter stages of the game— though Sam had not come back with the three-legs —he gave evidence that his " two " were capable of some tremendous punches. Both half backs, Jolley and Faulkner, were good, Seldom, indeed, could their performances be equalled—not even by the " two Macs" of stage fame (Macfat and Mac Lean. Of the three-quarters, the palm must go to Butterworth, whose display was solid throughout. Warder and Wilkinson also worked hard,hat lacked the finish of the little man Jones, though he man- aged some occasional smart things; on the whole exhibited some weak tendencies. The forwards gave good evidences of quality, but as I said before, there's room for lots of improvement. Conspicuous amongst them were Walker, Gayter, and Lightfoot, all of whom exhibited fine qualities, A SPARKLING EXHIBITION.

Runcorn's performance again,t Swinton.-11 points to three ! Look at it ! Think of it ! In my mind I am living the battle over again, only instead of seeing each separate detail and gaze upon the whole display and have but one word in which to express it—Finished ! Yes that is the only word that can adequately describe those quick passages, those bewildering melees, in which all appeared confusion, but which were all the outcome of per- fect organization, In the Wigan match certainly there was something wanting, but who would have thought the improvement would have come so quickly and at the same time so perfectly. Yot it has come : and come to stay ! Phoenix like the old Runcorn has died and out of the ashes there arises a new "hird"—the linnet.—Two slashing vic- tories within the week,and another to come for truly unless some one can work wonders in the ranks of the Widnes team by next Saturday the match be- tweed the "two sides of the silver streak" can hut have one result, But back to the battle. To what do the victors owe their triumph ; to unity, grit, and determination. Houghton was again himself, this time never a moment of anxiety did he give. The three-quarters all worked well, Butterworth, Warder, Wilkinson, and Jones—(yes oven Jones had recovered his nerve)—were all there. But it was Jolley perhaps who figured first and foremost. He was the figure in the centre around which the rest formed the glorious setting—and throughout the whole union one may look but they will not find a better display of drop kicking than that of Jolley. Faulkner what of him you ask—well lots— but only to mention one of the exhibits he 7,s,ye would speak of that bout of passing which resulted in the most brilliant position the game reached towards the close of the last half. The ether figure s in the "part" were Butterworth and Jones. Then the Runcorn pack— opposed as it was by as fine a pack as ever put heads together—who shall find fault with one of them, surely not I, Not a lazy man, not a wooden man, not a single dummy amongst them. It would not matter which was mentione-4 first--they all deserve that honour, and may they continue to deserve it is the hope of the " Scorcher." Now, what are we going to do with Widnes next Saturday ?—well, just romp over them, for the dis- play of the Widnes team—though of course they beat, Minoru on Saturday last—was streets inferior to that of any display Runcorn have given us yet. Go in and win Runcorn ; but go in with your eyes open, play every inch of the way. Don't let us have any holding cheap and there is but one result, the " hat trick."

TEN THOUSAND TROOPS FRONCINDLi TO BE

MOVED IN A WEEK. The Central News says on most reliab:e authority that in consequence of reptattd representations made to them from Natal, the Government have undertaken to move 10,000 troops from India to Natal within a week.

DEA.TH OF A CHESHIRE J.F. News arrived at Sandbach yesterday of the death of Mr. John Aaron Howard, J.P., for Cheshire, who died on Wednesday at Keigmet, Sussex. The deceased gentle- man was the owner of Breret3n Hall, formerly the seat of the Brereton family, famous in the Civil War. Be was one of the parties in the Howard will suit last year. Mr. Howard was 47 years of age, and leaves a wife and one son aged three years.
