VIOLENr STORM IN PARIS.

PARIS, THURSDAY. A violent thunderstorm burst over Paris shortly before nine last night. Rain, mingled with hailstones of re• markable sin,fell in torrents, and in half as hour many streets and boulevard 3 were transformed into so maay lakes.

DESTRUCTIVE OUTBREAK OF FIRE AT ST. HELENS.

An alarming and destructive fire broke out at the Globe Chemical Works, St. Helens, owned by the United Alkali Company, yesterday morning. At three o'clock the engine house was found en fire, and the fleors and woodwork being saturated with oil the flames spread rapidly, and soon the entire building was blazing furi- ously. The Corporation fire brigade, under Super- intendant Lyon attended with the steamer and hose reels, and found that the roof and two floors had fallen in. The extensive vitriol chambers, cased with wood,. and valuable oxidising plant, were situated within a few yards of the burning building, and the brigade, by desperate efforts, prevented their ignition. The engine house was cnmpletely gutted, and will necessitate a stoppage of workmen in that department for some days. It is not known how the fire originated, and the dam- age, the exteut of which has not yet been ascertained, is covered by insurance.

GREAT SANKEY SCHOOL BOARD. A meeting of the Great Sankey School Board Was- held on Saturday, there being present: Messrs. Halton, Perks, Wright and Kilshaw. Pending the election of chairman, the clerk took the chair. On the proposition of Mr. Wright seconded by Mr Perks. Mr. Halton was unanimously elected chair- man for the ensuing three years. A BUNCH OF VIOLETS. It wasn't much—hut this is how it happened. We were passing out of Waterloo Station on our way to Bournemouth ; sitting opposite to us was a working man's wife—she looked like a child wife. A baby was in her arms. Her husband, bronzed, youthful. kind, came to the carriage door to give an affectionate good-bye. Then he was missing a few ssconds, returned, and with a smile that meant worlds to the young wife, jest as the train moved off he threw in her lap a bunch of Vio'ets. It wasn't much, but the love-look on the wife's face was a full reward. 'Twas not the value of the Violets, but the theughtful love. HEAVY FAILURE OF A LOCAL GROCER. EXAMINATION OF MR. COLLIER. STOCK EXCHANGE LOSSES. Before Mr Registrar Bldg way at the Warrington Bank- ruptcy Court, on Friday, Mr. Walter Collier, at Runcorn, who has camel on an extensive grocery and provision business at Runcorn, Widnes, Warrington, and Frod- sham, appeared for his first public examination in bank- ruptcy.—Mr. Unsworth, solicitor. represented the trustee, and Mr. F. R. Roberts appeared on behalf e, f Mr. 10t,ert Henshall. one of the creditors. The state- ment of assetc, and liabilities shows that the liabilities I are expected t rank at £8,524 13s 01, 411:1 the assets I £2,445 193 61, leaving a deficiency of £6,078 133 61. The oebtor was examined at considerable length by Mr. Potter (Assistant Official Receiver). The debtor re- markei that the statement he made to the Official Re- ceiver was correct. In it the bankrupt states that in 1889 he took over, froth his father's executors, the shop, 6, Church-street, Runcorn, practically without capital, the assets eq railing the liabilities. Ha subsequently opened various branch shops and a coal deco', ; He first became aware of bis insolvency in March, 1998. has since contracted a few debts now owing ; has speculated on the Stock Exchange for the last five years and admits he has lost about £4,000 mainly during the last two years. His 'wife claims about one half of the furniture at 6, Church-street, Runcorn, as being gifts from friends, and his mother claims furniture at 17, ' Church-street: In reply to further questions the debtor stated that when he took over his father's business he took over a liability of £5OO, a legacy due to his father's wife. That formed part of the liabilities which about balanced with the assets when he took over the business. By Mr. Potter: Why didn't you pay the money off long ago P—Debtor: I continued to pay interest on it at the rate of five per cent.—Did your mother express a wish to have the interest instead of the capital ? Debtor: In the will I was given the option to pay the interest.—l see nothing in the will that you should con- tinue paying interest P—Debtor : It was always under- stood that I cauld pay interest on the money.—Mr. Potter: What are the causes of your insolvency ?—The Debtor : Loses on the Stock Exchange and my bankers finding out that I had speculated. I had a cheque dis- honoured through it and my credit fell. You can hardly complain of the action of the bank when they found you were losing on the Stock Exchange. When did you commence transactions on the Stock Exchange P—About the beginning of 1896. • You say in your statement: I have speculated for the last 5 years and lost about £4,000 mainly during the last two years. is that answer true ?—The Debtor : Yes. Had you any money accumulated in your business to speculate with P—The Debtor: A man doing a large business has always a good floating balance. But it wasn't yours to invest P—ln plain English it , v ain't. I Llaveh't these been spcculations pure and simp'e from the very first ?—Yes. Up to when did you continue P—About the end of August. ' When did you become aware of your insolvency ? About March or April 1899. Considering your estate only shows 6s in the £ how do you account for your not becoming aware of your in- solvency before ?—Tbat is only an estimate of the . estate. Considering that your losses on the Stock Exchange were incurred over twelve menthe ago were you not aware in the summer of 1898 that you could not pay your debts ?—At that time my credit was very good. I had a large turnover and there was nothing to lead me to think that it would not come out all right. As you had no capital to start with was it not clear that you were hopelessly insolvent in September 1898. From what source did you expect to recoup yourself ?—I gave up speculating and turned my attention to business. I knew what the business Could do and I felt quite capable of bringing it through all right. When did you first discover that your liabilities exceeded your assets P—lt would be in March or May. Is it not a fact that you have contracted the greater part of your liabilities with full knowledge of your in- solvency ?—Well a fair proportion. What justification can you suggest for going on trad- ing with a knowledge of your insolvency P—Practically I have traded for the last four or five months with the Lull consent of the largest creditors. Did these creditors know of your losses ?—I told them I had lost money on the Stock Exchange and said I should pull it up with the business. How do you account for having such a large number of unsecured creditors as 170 ?—ln a trade of this size you are bound to trade direct and you have quite 150 or 160 articles. How many creditors out of these 172 did you consult as to the desirabifitv of continuing business when son were :insolvent ?—About 15 of the largest ores. Can you give the total losses on the Stock Exchange ; may I take it itwas about £5,000 P—Between £4,000 and 5,000. Have you opened any branch shops this year ?—Yes in February, at Bridge Foot, Warrington. Considering your enormous losses how do you justify your conduct in further extending the business ?—I didn't expect that the business would have been brought down Did you enter into a ten years' lease for the Bridge Foot property, and this with the full knowledge that you had dropped £5,000 on the Stock Exchange ?—Yes ; but my credit was not bad in February. A firm compelled me to give up the Bridge Foot shop, and after I bad done so there was a rush of creditors that compelled me to come into Court. The Assistant Official tieceiver questioned the debtor at some length as to the furniture which his mother and wife claimed. He said the greater portion of it was left by his father. In answer to further questions the debtor stated that ' he took stock about three times a year at the branches and at the Church-street shop very seldom. Why didn't you make some effort to ascertain how you stood financially P—lt was always my intention to put it on a thorough basis of book-keeping but it was never done. Why did you pay off two people on the 24th July just before filing your petition ?—I was asked for the money. They were employees. Will you give their names ?—Mr. Edward Roach and Mr. John Basnett. The Assistant Official Receiver said he must ask for an account in detail showing the losses on the Stock Ex- change and an amended deficiency account based on a profit and loss account. Mr. Unsworth then cross-examined the witness, who stated that he bad 8 or 9 writs served upon him befere he filed his petition. He paid two and tbe others were arranged for. Mr. Unsworth proceeded to question the witness as to certain shares, and the debtor admitted that he had no intention of taking up stock per- manently. The examination was adjourned until November 3rd, and in the meantime Mr. Collier was instructed to pre- pare tbe statements mentioned by the Assistant Official Receiver. BANKRUPTCY OF A LOCAL BUILDER. Herbert Rawling Ezerd, who recently resided and carried on business as a joiner and builder at Victoria-road, Poulton-with-Fearnhead, was further examined with respect to his bankruptcy at the Warrington Court OH Friday before Mr. Regis- trar Ridgway. The Official Receiver's state- ment showed that the liaoilities were expected to rank at £3,849 194 4d. The assets are estimated by the bankrupt to realise £2,046 16s 31, leaving a deficiency of £1,803 3s F. R. Roberts appeared for the debtor, Mr. Grant of Manchester for Mr. Henshall, one of the creditors, and Mr. Longland also for creditors. In answer to the Assistant c fficial Reeeiver,the bankrupt stated that it was on the 6th July when he first agreed to hand over goods to Mr. Henshall in fulfilment of an agreement. He valued the goods and plant which he handed over at £382, from which he received £235. Asked to explain why he should do such a thing, the debtor said he could hardly answer that question, but he riould have received more money from Mr. Henshall if he had continued building. He denied the suggestion of Mr. Potter that the object was to supply Mr. Henshall with security for about £l5O, and stated that Mr. Henshall in addition to advancing the sum mentioned also paid £3O to the Railway Company for carriage of goods. He was aware that by handing over his plant to Mr. Henshall he had reduced his assets by at least £l2O. Whatever he had done was under the advice of his lawyer. Further questioned by Mr. Potter as to whether the sale of the plant to Mr Henshall did not prevent him from continuing his business, the bankrupt, stated that Mr. Henshall allowed him the right to use the plant after he had sold it to him. He denied that there was anything behind the transaction,and said that it was a sale out and out. It was part of the arrange- ment that he should be allowed to use the plant. He continued to use the plant until the 13th July. Mr. Potter ; You have scheduled over 50 unsecured creditors, and they complain that they are left out in the cold, as you have made over your assets to Mr. Henshall. The Bankrupt: What about the equities in the pro- perties ? Mr. Potter : They don't attach much value to them. The Bankrupt proceeded to explain bow he came to sign a mertage deed which purported to have advanced him £BOO on the security of some property. '

THE FILESMITIIS' STRIKE AT WAR RINGTON. This strike still continues without any prospect of settlement. A deputation of the men waited upon Messrs. Thewlis and Griffiths, Limited this week to see if a pacific settlement could be arrived at, but without any satisfactory result being obtained. Jhe men are receiving substantial assist- ance from numerous sources and are in a position to bold out for some considerable time. Fresh appeals are being issued in various parts of the country and these are endorsed by the Sheffield Trades Council and signed by Mr. C. Hobson, J.P, president, and Mr. S. Uttley, J.P., secretary. At the present time both parties to the dispute show no inclination to yield, and the struggle promises to be an unusually long one. It seems a dispute in which arbitration might be introduced with advan- tage.
